About the Town
SETTLED:
1718
INCORPORATED:
August 1, 1728
TYPE OF GOVERNMENT:
Town Meeting
LOCATION:
North Central Massachusetts, bordered by Townsend on
the north, Shirley on the east, Leominster and Lancaster
on the south, and Fitchburg and Ashby on the west. It is
about 47 miles from Boston and 29 miles from Worcester.
COUNTY:
Worcester
ELEVATION AT TOWN HALL:
Approximately 570 ft. above mean sea level
LAND AREA:
26.63 Square Miles
TOTAL AREA:
29 Square Miles
TOWN ROADS:
76.97 Miles
STATE ROADS:
6 Miles

===PAGE 6===
Board of Selectmen
Board of Selectmen: left to right - Rudolph Masella, Chairman Normand J. Thompson and Sherwood G. Winchester.
The Bicentennial Year 1976 found Lunenburg confronting many of the same problems we have been struggling
with in recent years -- more State mandates, more Federal controls, more infringements upon our Home Rule. The
feelings of pride and greater appreciation of our country brought about by the many events of the celebration during
the year, unfortunately, did little to solve the problems of the overburdened taxpayer. Ever increasing costs, re-
quests for services, demands by State and Federal government, have made the task of administering local government
an even more difficult task. The question of how we can best guide our Town, giving the best possible services at
the lowest possible costs, is a never ending problem.
Your Board of Selectmen find that more than ever this type of problem takes more and more of their time and
that the only reason Lunenburg has come as far as it has, as successfully as it has, is because of the numbers of dedi-
cated people who are willing to give freely of their time and talents to wrestle with the Town's problems. We applaud
each and every one of them -- Lunenburg would not be the desirable Town it is without them.
Solid waste disposal is a perfect example of the problems confronting the Town. We know that to handle our
solid waste under the present governmental regulations will be very costly. We also know that given the right condi-
tion it could be less costly, more effective and could help our energy problems. The question is "How can we make
this ideal solution come about without creating an ineffective agency which will ultimately strangle US with unreason-
able higher costs ?". Regionalization appears to be the answer to many of these problems, but is fraught with the
perils of uncontrollable bureaucracy.
Like it or not, certain State and Federal monies become available to US through the Montachusett Regional
Planning Commission. We must make every effort to see that this Commission serves US and that we do not end up
serving the Commission. We must be ever vigilant in preventing the possibility that a bureaucracy will overrun US.
This will mean a closer scrutiny of the activities of this agency and Lunenburg should be in the forefront in this effort.
Your Selectmen have continued their involvement with the Massachusetts Selectmen's Association and the
Massachusetts League of Cities & Towns in an attempt to workwith other communities with similar problems in order
to resolve some of them. We are no longer an isolated community and by communicating with and working with our
fellow communities, we have a better chance of solving individual as well as collective problems.
We will not go into the details of all the functions of the Board -- elsewhere in this Report the various depart-
ments will tell you in greater detail than we could here what has happened this year. This Board is proud to have
had the opportunity to serve the Town of Lunenburg.
-4-
===PAGE 7===
Board of Assessors
Board of Assessors: left to right - Chairman Bernard J. Paton, John F. Nicoll and William F. Reed.
The Board of Assessors wishes to report that 1976 appears to have been a slow year for new construction. The
majority of the construction during the past year has been additions to existing housing and additional outbuildings.
We would estimate the additional valuation coming into being during 1976 to be under a million dollars. Included
in this figure are a number of new house completions which were assessed on a percentage basis last year due to their
being in progress on January 1, 1976.
We wish to report that in compliance with the "Sudbury Decision" and the Commissioner of Corporations and
Taxation directives to bring the valuations of the Town to "full and fair cash value", we will, as of January 1, 1977,
increase all valuations by 10%. This increase will include all land, real estate and commercial property in the Town.
We are obligated to carry out the plan submitted to the Massachusetts Supreme Judicial Court by the Town. This plan
was outlined in our report to the Town in the 1975 Town Report. This action will have the effect of increasing the
total valuation of the Town, however, it will also lower the tax rate if the Town budgets were to remain the same as
last year, and the total amount of money a person would pay for taxes would also remain the same.
The Board of Assessors is requesting funds through an Article at the forthcoming Annual Town Meeting. We
wish to change our present method of recording land transactions in the Assessors' Office and acquiring topographical
maps made of a size to be usable in the field. Our problem is that the aerial maps now being used to show property
lines are becoming worn from twenty-six years of use. Each sale is now drawn onto the existing photographs by hand
in ink and the lots are described by the owner's name and area of the tract of land.
We propose to have topographical maps drawn from our photomaps, reduced to a manageable size and a new
numerical system of recording ownership established. Each tract of land in Lunenburg would have a number, a card
file would show the owner's name. In the event of a sale a new card would be filled out and the change would be
complete.
The existing photomaps would be available for viewing of property, but would not be maintained as to future
changes. We do feel that topographical maps in a convenient size to be used in the field have long been a necessity
to assist the Assessors in carrying out their duties.
-5-
===PAGE 8===
The Board of Assessors wishes to remind taxpayers of certain exemptions that have been enacted concerning the
value of residential property, ownedand occupied as a domicile by persons in various groups for whom the payment
of taxes could constitute an undue burden. These exemptions may not exceed certain specific amounts. If a taxpayer
feels that he qualifies for any of the following exemptions, he should contact the Assessors' Office and request an ap-
plication which lists all the requirements that must be met in order to qualify.
Section 17
- Widows, minors whose fathers are deceased, and persons over 70 - $2,000 in valuation
or a tax of $175, whichever is greater.
Section 22
- Disabled or wounded veterans, or their wives, widows or parents - $2,000 to $10,000 or
a tax from $175 to $875, whichever is greater depending on degree of disability.
Section 22E
- Veterans with yearly certificate from Veterans' Administration indicating 100%disability.
Section 37
- Blind persons - $5,000 valuation or a tax of $437.50, whichever is greater.
Section 41
- Persons aged 70 or over - $4,000 valuation or a tax of $350, whichever is greater.
Section 41A
- Tax deferral for certain persons 65 years of age or over with gross receipts of under
$20,000 annually to place the taxes on his domicile and one acre of land on a deferred
payment plan, the taxes to be paid upon settlement of the estate plus 8% interest.
Section 42
- Unmarried widows of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000
valuation, or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Section 43
- Minor orphans of police officers or firefighters killed in the line of duty - $8,000 in valu-
ation, or a tax of $700, whichever is greater.
Clause 61A Section 9 - Provides for assessment of agricultural or horticultural land at value based upon its agri-
cultural or horticultural use.
Planning Board
Planning Board efforts in 1976 were concentrated primarily in the following areas: (1) The study of the up-
dating of The Rules and Regulations of the Planning Board of the Town of Lunenburg Governing The Subdivision of
Land and the approval of these new regulations on September 15, 1976. Our requirements are now in agreement with
State standards under Subdivision Control Law, Chapter 41, Sections 81K to 81GG. (2) The initiation of a new up-
date of the Protective By-law of the Town of Lunenburg. This is in response to a State mandated requirement under
Chapter 808 of the Acts of 1975 requiring that all cities and towns of the Commonwealth have individual zoning by-
laws in conformance with said Chapter 808 by June of 1978. The review of our By-law will be a continuing study in
1977. (3) The review and final approval in April of the Laurelwood Subdivision off Chase Road. (4) The week to
week approval of subdivision lots not requiring subdivision approval.
Additionally, in response to a request from the Growth and Development Committee for a growth and develop-
ment policy for Lunenburg, this Board recommended that Lunenburg should: a. maintain moderate growth; b. en-
courage growth of light, clean industry; C. encourage growth in established growth areas; d. enact zoning that
would be structured to the capability of the land, taking into consideration the water supply and the ability of the
land to absorb sewage. Finally, the Planning Board approved the revised HUD Floodplain Map of Lunenburg sub-
mitted by the Department of Housing and Urban Development. This approval came after lengthy study with our wet-
lands intern, Mr. Richard Defieux.
-6-
===PAGE 9===
The statistics for Lunenburg suggest that Lunenburg continues to grow despite the recession. Nationally, the
housing industry has been depressed with fewer "housing starts" than in previous years. However, the building per-
mits issued for Lunenburg indicate that housing starts not only have kept pace with the past, but also have increased
by four. Likewise there has been an increase in lots approved -- 18 more than in 1975.

===PAGE 10===
Park Commission
The 1976 Summer Program was conducted under the direction of Richard Stachowicz and was highlighted by a
very successful Muscular Dystrophy Yard Sale at the Center and Wallis Park playgrounds. The playground activities
at the Center were under the direction of Chris DeFeo for most of the season and her originality made the program
very exciting for the children in attendance. The Wallis Park program is also well attended, but there is concern in
that an older age group is attending the program and has posed a serious problem to playground instructors. The Arts
and Crafts program was conducted very capably by Stephanie Markham and Cindy St. Arnaud. The Town Beach saw
a decrease in attendance this year due in large part to the absence of a raft. The swim classes, however, were very
well attended and Gerry Beloin and his staff are to be commended.
Although most of the Park Commission programshave been running smoothly, it has become more and more ap-
parent that we are in competition with the area cities and the State for personnel to operate our summer programs.
Our current wage scale for lifeguards and playground instructors puts US at a serious disadvantage in securing qualified
people as we are unable to match what these other agencies are offering.
The replacement of aging playground equipment is now becoming a problem. It is hoped that the annual re-
placement of at least one or more of these items could be accomplished in the future. Since the removal of the old
quonset hut at Wallis Park, there has been a need for some kind of shelter where tables can be set up for the Arts
and Crafts program.
During the year the Park Commissioners met with representatives of the School Department and updated the
Mutual Use of Facilities Agreement. This will now be an annual process in orderto insure the best use of the facil-
ities of both departments for the benefit of the public. During 1976 the Commissioners considered the possibility of
expanding the summer playground activities so as to include a program at Normand Park similar to those conducted
at the Center and Wallis Park playgrounds. If enough interest in such a program is shown over the coming year, the
Commissioners will try to include such a program in the budget so that it could begin in the summer of 1978.
The most notable accomplishment this year for the Park Commission was the completion of a new foundation for
the Town Beach House. The work was done by the John Morin Company of Fitchburg under the direction of Raymond
Bourque of that company. The new foundation will hopefully add many more years of usefulness to this building.
Additional work has yet to be done to the rest of the structure and should be accomplished before opening this season.
Finally, the Park Commissioners would like to thank all who have helped make our programs successful and a
word of thanks to Bob Garneau and Greg Bingham who have done an excellent job of maintaining the parks and play-
grounds for the Town's use and enjoyment.
Park Commission: left to right - Park Superintendent Robert A. Garneau, Chairman
Richard J. Bishop and Armand E. St. Arnaud. Member not present, R. Wilfred Vowles.
-8-
===PAGE 11===
Cemetery Commission
During 1976 the Cemetery Commissioners met as scheduled throughout the year. Two equipment purchases were
made in 1976 one beinga new truck cab and chassis (the old body was repaired and painted before installation on
the new chassis) and the other purchase was a new riding tractor mower to replace the old one. In the spring, both
Cemeteries were seeded, fertilized and lime applied to enhance the grounds and insure healthy vegetation, and the
grounds were given special grooming in preparation for Memorial Day.
Throughout the year much maintenance work was done including the repair of old monuments to preserve the
appearance of the Cemeteries. To protect the monuments from damage through the use of conventional weed cutting
implements, a new monofilament weed cutter was purchased thus permitting closer trimming near monuments, trees and
bushes without any damage. In continuing the maintenance program, the road hedges were trimmed and some paint-
ing was done to the Cemetery Department office building. The following table lists the names of persons buried in
Lunenburg Cemeteries in 1976:

===PAGE 12===
CEMETERY RULES AND REGULATIONS
(As Approved at the Annual Town Meeting, April 3, 1976)
1. All burials and removals therein shall be conducted under the supervision of the Superintendent.
1A. The Board of Cemetery Commissioners may, by Deed made and executed in such manner and form as it may pre-
scribe, convey to any person, the sole and exclusive right of burial in any lot in the Town Cemeteries, in accord-
ance with the following schedule of prices, provided, however, that the Town shall provide or set aside a suitable
portion as a public burial place for use of inhabitants, free of charge.
Price Per Grave . $25.00
2. No interments shall be made except in a concrete vault.
2A. No burials shall take place on Sundays or Holidays.
3. Notrees shall be planted and onlysuch shrubs may be planted are approved by the Commissioners. The plant-
ing of such shrubs must be done under the supervision of the Superintendent.
4. Charges for burials shall be as follows:
Regular Burials

Crematory Remains
15.00
(Dec. 1st to Apr. 1st)
100.00

25.00

The Commissioners reserve the right to makea charge for snow removal in connection with winter burials, said charge
to be in additionto any burial charge in effect at the time, and to make additional charges in any other case where
it becomes necessary to remove snow or ledge or open frozen ground.
5. Perpetual Care shall consist of the following:
A. Periodic mowing of grass.
B. Raking and removing grass and dead leaves.
C. Filling and reseeding graves.
6. Any individual having a right or rights of burial in any lotshall provide for Perpetual Care ofsuch lot by paying
a sum of money as hereinafter stipulated.
7. The charges for Perpetual Care shall be as follows:
1st Grave - $75.00 and Each Additional Grave - $25.00.
8. All individuals requesting a grave lot, or before exercising a right of burial in a lot shall, before a grave is op-
ened, fill out and sign, in duplicate, an application therefor and submit the same to the Superintendent. Such forms
shall be entitled "Cemetery Application" and shall be in such form the Cemetery Commissioners deem necessary.
The same may be obtained from the Commissioners or the Superintendent.
9. All money due on a grave lot shall be paid before opening a grave unless satisfactory arrangements have been
made with the Cemetery Commissioners for their payment.
10. In the event of a default in the payment of agreed charges, the Commissioners reserve theright to reassign any
unoccupied grave lot.
11. No monument allowed on less than two grave lot, only marker on one grave lot.
12. All fees due Cemetery Department must be paid before setting of stones.
13. The Commissioners shall have the right to refuse to assign or convey a burial lot to any person not an inhabi-
tant of the Town of Lunenburg.

Library Trustees

Library Trustees: left to right - Librarian John E. Ramsay, Barbara T. Murray, Barbara W. Brown, Janet M. Cragin
and Chairman Robert S. Tapply. Absent when picture was taken were John J. Boursy and Beverly A. Mulholland.
Use of the Library increased significantly over the past year with circulation of all materials up by 5%. A com-
bination of factors contributed to this expanded utilization of facilities. In the children's area alone, there was a
7½% increase in the circulation of children's items (books, magazines, toys, puzzles, filmstrips and records). The
bi-weekly preschool story hours on Fridayshave been enthusiastically attended and appreciated. A very active sum-
mertime reading program resulted in the reading of 1,288 children's books, with special events including a showing
of the movie "King Kong" (old version), a magic show, a stage make-up exhibition, and a concluding' program by
the Little Red Wagon, a touring drama group from the University of New Hampshire. This last activity was funded
by the Friends of the Ritter Memorial Library, who contributed countlesshours inhelping make this Library the active
cultural and educational center it has now become.
The Friends of the Ritter Memorial Library helped plan and carry out several children's programs including Sat-
urday morning movies during the winter, a Children's Arts Festival (art displays by children, songfest, puppet show,
and refreshments), a Halloween party in which local artist, Murl Daniels, demonstrated his skills in stage make-up
and the Friends taught children how to make creative masks, story hours for the children at the Lunenburg Arts Fes-
tival, and a two-phase workshop on making Christmas decorations from dough ornaments. Such activities have in-
creased the use of the Library's facilities, provided a lot of fun for the youngsters, and have helped break down for
everyone the illusion of a library's being just a staid depository for dusty old volumes.
In the adult realm, the Library has continued its Sunday night service of showing top-rate films to area peo-
ple. Among those viewed in 1976 were Rebecca, The Bicycle Thief, 400 Blows, Lost Horizon, Hamlet, and Suspi-
cion. These showings have been well attended, and have required virtually no town money for the program. Large
window shades to facilitate showing films at the Library during the day as well as at night have been contributed by
the Friends of the Library. They have also continued their popular Wednesday morning "Interest Series", featuring
lectures and exhibits on topics such as wills and probate, cosmetics and hair care, funeral planning, making fabric
flowers and calico wreaths, and information on loans, savings and checking accounts. Each topic, no matter what
the weather, has drawn consistently large and interested audiences.
Despite a practically frozen budget on service categories, this year has been significant in the obtaining of many
materials not previously owned by this Library, without directly costing the Town anything. The Library qualified for
State aid monies totalling over $2,780.00. Two unexpected grants, one for $500.00 worth of reference books, and a
booming $3,220.00 federal grant for various materials, enabled the purchase of many sorely needed books, office
-12-
===PAGE 15===
equipment, library shelving, and items such as records, cassettes, filmstrips, and children's puzzles and toys, all of
which amazingly have never been available here before. All this was accomplished without any direct town funding.
These sources never were available in the past, and not expected in the future, but were certainly much appreciated.
For the statistically inclined, the following indicates the circulation of materials over the past year: Adult
Books 19, 133 (678 paperbacks) (106 interlibrary loans); Records 668; Talking Books 4; Magazines 373; Pamphlets
39; Children's Books 22, 720; Children's Magazines 111; Audio Visual Material 143; Children's Records 162; and Films
112. The total circulation of materials for 1976 was 44,
An interesting way to view these figures, devoid of any consideration of the many non-book services provided
by the Library, is to determine the amount that people in the area would have spent to buy the books they read last
year at the Library. At the list price charged in bookstores, the total cost would have been around $695,000, or about
$85 per person. In fiscal year 1976-1977, the amount spent per capita will be $6.39, for a savings to our people of
some 92½%. Considering these figures, plus the myriad cultural and informational facilities available here, the Ritter
Memorial Library stands out as one of the best overall bargains available anywhere these days!
Development & Industrial Commission
It has been the thinking of the Development & Industrial Commission over the past several years that water,
its supply (quantity and quality), distribution and storage, is of primary concern to the future growth and develop-
ment within the Town of Lunenburg. It is fairly common knowledge that any potential industrial development has
been discouraged by lack of water. The Lunenburg Water District which operates separately and independent from
Town government, services two main areas of Town the Center and the Whalom District. These areas are residen-
tial and constitute our highest population density. This Commission is firm in its stand that there should be no in-
dustrial development within the confines of the present Water District. Industry, as we see it, should be included
in the Town's overall growth plan, but it must be contained away from population centers. For these reasons, we
have earmarked two areas of Town as future industrial sites one in the extreme south end of Town along Leominster-
Shirley Road and the other in the north end of Town on Route 13 (Chase Road).
The Development & Industrial Commission's concept of industry for Lunenburg is small and light those firms
that would only be interested in water for fire protection and not for process use. These would provide their
own sewerage disposal. They would be the type of industry that is clean and noiseless and who would desire location
in a small, well-landscaped industrial park. They would draw their employees from neighboring cities and thereby
their work force would primarily be commuters who reside outside the Town. It is our contention that keeping in-
dustry out of Lunenburg will in no way alter our growth picture. As our neighboring cities and towns develop in-
dustry, we will pick up our proportionate share of new residents.
The main thrust behind this desire to develop industry within Town is to control growth. By developing two
potential industrial sites in the outlying district and perhaps changing the Protective By-law to include industrial
zoning, we stand a chance to limit residential growth in these areas. Despite the changes in lot size in outlying
areas, there has been little curtailment of growth in these areas. In fact, the contrary has happened -- new homes
are springing up in areas that are too close to potential industry. This can only lead to future problems between
homeowners and industry. Without some type of zoning change, neither the homeowner nor industry know where
they stand, and it isn't fair to either.
Along with the concept of developing industrial sites to control growth, it is our feeling that these parcels
of land be buffered with large tracts of conservation property. This will limit the size of industry and prevent its
gradual spread toward population centers. Our largest existing industry is located in the south end of Town with its
own water supply and there is the potential of Leominster water being run into an industrial park on the Leominster-
Lunenburg Line, Therefore, it would seem a plan for zoning regulations and conservation buffering be formulated
now so that the Town is in control of the situation.
It is our belief that through proper planning, industry can become a key factor in controlling overall growth
within Lunenburg so that we maintain our image as a rural bedroom community. Our views are not focused in rapid
uncontrolled growth or stopping growth, but rather on controlling our growth rate so that we might protect and im-
prove upon what we already have.

Town Nursing Service
0
Nursing Staff: left to right - Jean E. Hobbs, Shirley M. Carpenter, Janet N. Pace and Marie B. Richards.
The Lunenburg Public Health Nursing Service, a division of the Board of Health, offers a comprehensive com-
munity health program to the people of Lunenburg. This includes Visiting Nurse Services, the School Health Program
and Board of Health Nursing follow-up. To perform these services four registered nurses are on the staff: Janet N.
Pace, Nursing Supervisor, Shirley M. Carpenter, Jean E. Hobbsand Marie B. Richards. Barbara D. Eley is employed
part-time as the Agency's secretary.
The goal of the Nursing Service is to provide a comprehensive community health program designed to meet the
total health needs of Lunenburg; via care in their home and programs in the community. To ensure that this goal is
met, an evaluation of the Agency is done annually by the Professional Advisory Committee, Chaired by Dr. Heimberg.
This Committee also meets periodically to review and recommend practices and programs of the Agency.
Annually, the Nursing Service is surveyed and certified by the Federal Government so as to be eligible to care
for Medicare, Medicaid, and other private insurance patients. The fee for a Nursing Visit remains $10.50, either
through patient or third-party payment; although no person is ever refused care because of inability to pay. The serv-
ices offered to the residents of the community are skilled nursing care in their homes by the Agency Nursing Staff and
under the guidance of their physician and by contractual arrangement, Home Health Aides and Medical Social Serv-
ices. The Agency also has a contract with Burbank Hospital's Home Health Care Program which offers supportive serv-
ices such as physical, occupational and speech therapy.
Well-Baby Conferences are held monthly through the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health. Dr. Joos,a Pedia-
trician from Bolton, is at each clinic where physical examinations and immunizations are offered free-of-charge to
infants and preschoolers. This service is meant to supplement that of a family's regular physician by increasing the
health services available to Lunenburg children.
Well-Oldster Clinics are held monthly at the United Parish Building for people of Lunenburg, 55 years of age
and over. Blood pressure checks are done regularly with urine screening and blood sugar tests done periodically.
During 1976 the Dental Hygienist from Nashoba Associated Boards of Health was present to do oral examinations.
Dr. James Dawson, a podiatrist, discussed foot problems and Mary Gardner, a nutritionist with the New England
Dairy Council gave a presentation on nutrition for the elderly.
-14-
===PAGE 17===
The fall of 1976 saw the Nursing Staff involved in the National Swine Flu Immunization Program. Clinics
were run for the elderly, chronically ill, Town employees and the general population. Before the program ended,
975 people had been immunized, approximately 17% of the eligible population.
The following is a summary of the Nursing activities for 1976: Bedside Nursing Visits, 074 (689 to persons
over 65 and 385 to persons under 65); Maternal and Child Health Visits, 15; Health Supervision, 271; Visits to School
Children, 56; Home Health Aide Visits, 365. Also, 5 Well Child Conferences were held and 23 Well Oldster Clinics,
12 at the Pearl Brook Apartments and 11 at the United Parish Building.
Housing Authority
Housing Authority: left to right - Executive Secretary Eileen M. Alleva, Reverend
Richard W. Dupee, Richard I. White, Chairman Ann P. Hall, Josephine C. Meloon,
Arthur J. Brockelman and Mildred E. Sprowson of the Tenants' Association.
The Lunenburg Housing Authority continues to operate the Pearl Brook Housing for the Elderly located at 131
White Street. The financing for the Pearl Brook Apartments changed somewhat this December as we became part of
the long-term borrowing instituted by the State to replace the annual sale of short-term notes, Hopefully, this will
avoid what has become in recent years the uncertainty encountered in a changing market.
Mrs. Molly Sprowson was elected President of the Pearl Brook Tenants' Association succeeding Mr. Robert
Cunningham. Mrs. Sprowson thus becomes the first woman to hold this position. The Association President attends
all Housing Authority meetings and is the principal liaison between the tenants and the Authority members.
Full occupancy of the forty-eight units is maintained at all times. The requirements for occupancy are that
applicants be at least 62 years of age and have an income for single persons of not more than $4,200.00 and for
couples of not more than $4,800.00. Rents are variable in that no more than 25% of income can be charged for
rent which includes heat, water and electricity.
We invite anyone who is interested to inquire at the Housing Authority Office where our Executive Secretary,
Mrs. Alleva, will be available to answer questions. Income limits are subject to change as a request to raise our
present limits is now before the Department of Community Affairs.
Tenants participate and enjoy the many activities held throughout the year and take an active part in com-
munity affairs.
-15-
===PAGE 18===
Council on Aging
The Council on Aging's proudest accomplishment of 1976 has been the inauguration of the "Meals on Wheels"
Program. The program officially began in October, delivering hot meals to the elderly shut-ins, three days a week.
From Octoberuntil the end of December a total of 335 meals have been served to an average of ten persons, on each
of the days. During these first three months of the program a total of 709 miles were travelled by an eager group of
volunteers, supervised by Mrs. Doris Carrier. The Council is most grateful to the following volunteers: Marie Gibbs,
Terry Chapdelaine, Edith Carey, Kathy Draskoczy, Tona Xenakis and Mrs. Carrier for their interest in donating their
time for such a worthwhile effort.
The purpose of instituting this program is two-fold -- to accommodate an elderly shut-in, living alone, by pro-
viding a hot, nutritious meal at a maximum cost of fifty cents per meal, and by having a cheery visitor come into
their home and share a few minutes of what would otherwise have been a very lonely day. With the experience of
knowing that the program has been a complete success in a three month period, the Council is now planning to expand
the "Meals on Wheels" to include fifteen persons, five days a week, beginning with the next fiscal year (July 1).
Success in a venture of this nature can only be attained with the cooperation of the School Committee, the School
Lunch Program and personnel, the Montachusett Nutrition Project, all of the volunteers, and the Lunenburg Public
Health Nursing Service.
With the planned expansion program, volunteers, both men and women, young or old, are desperately needed.
Anyone interested in serving as a volunteer may contact Mrs. Doris Carrier, Beal Street at 582-9959. Referrals and
requests may be made by calling the United Parish House office at 582-6572 from 9AM until 1PM Monday through
Friday. It should be understood, however, that any requests made must be on the basis of physical need only, since
not everyone is eligible for this program. Evaluations for such requests are made by the Lunenburg Public Health
Nursing Service.
Another accomplishment of the Council was initiating the mailing of an informative Newsletter, reaching ap-
proximately 950 Senior Citizens, on a quarterly basis. The purpose of this Newsletter is to keep the retired persons
informed of the many benefits regarding Social Security, insurance, programs and discounts offered by various busi-
nesses in the area.
The Fourth Annual Christmas Dinner was enjoyed by the 139 persons in attendance at the High School and was
followed by a musical program under the direction of Donald Adams. The Council sponsored two bus trips during the
year, one to Gloucester and the other a foliage through the Mohawk Trail. Both trips were well attended. The
Council was saddened by the loss of Roger W. Jones, who passed away in July, and regrets the resignation of
Howard O. Russell.
Council on Aging: left to right -Lucienne Chapdelaine, Reverend Richard W. Dupee, Marcella W. Peed, Arthur J.
Brockelman, Chairman Martha L. Molaghan, Alfred C. Gilchrest, Doris M. Carrier and Mark G. Thatcher. Absent
when picture was taken were Jean E. Hobbs, Thomas Passios, Beatrix F. Donnelly and Eino A. Liloiva.

Conservation Commission
The Lunenburg Conservation Commission met twenty-four times in regular meetings this year and went on ap-
proximately twenty inspection tours of problems relating to the Wetlands Act. Potential purchasers of homes or land
for a house lot should be aware that such an Act exists. Involvement in securing the proper permits and developing
conditions imposed by this Act are expensive and time consuming. During the Annual Town Meeting of 1975, the
Town approved the appropriation of $2000.00 to secure a Wetlands Survey.
For general information, it is the responsibility of a builder and/or property owner to contact the Conservation
Commission for a determination under the Wetlands Protection Act. Generally, any proposed construction, recon-
struction or alteration which could result in removal, filling, dredging or alteration of land within 100 feet of any
creek, river, stream, pond, lake, swamp or fresh water wetland, may require the filing of a written notice by cer-
tified mail to the Conservation Commission.
Thanks to the Audubon Society and their Environmental Intern Program, the Commission was ableto secure the
services of Mr. Richard Defieux who is trained in geological surveys. The results of this survey are maps of the
surficial water resources, geological water resources, a ground water favorability map and an updated and more
accurate HUD Flood Plain Map.
These mapshavebeen put to good use not only by the Conservation Commission, but alsoby the Planning Board
and the Water Study Committee. The report made by Mr. Defieux is very detailed and accurate. The Commission
is thankful for an organization such as the Audubon Society for making such a program available.
Our water resources are invaluable and irreplaceable. The availability of the information in this survey will
help US protect and conserve the water that keeps Lunenburg green and beautiful.
Conservation Commission: Seated left to right - Chairman Raymond G. Chapdelaine, Harold D.
Harley and Nancy 1. Sholl. Standing left to right - William H. Naylor, Russell N. Harvey
and Henry A. Anderson. Absent when picture was taken was Edward H. Hall, Jr.

Personnel Board
Personnel Board: left to right - Chairman Charles F. Miller, Frank H. DeBettencourt,
Jacqueline A. Keeler, Robert P. McNally and Michael J. Freel.
This past year the Personnel Board was pleased to be able to maintain fair and attractive wage and benefit pro-
grams for our Town employees in the face of rapidly increasing costs of all Town operations. The Board makes an at-
tempt to provide a rewarding and pleasant atmosphere for our employees and also helps to maintain a cost basis under
which various Town administrators can operate without excessive cost to the taxpayers. We appreciate our loyal
employees hard work and cooperative effort towards this end.
The Board also appreciates the helpful input obtained on a regular basis from our Town Department Heads.
Their information and suggestions, along with data they gather, are an invaluable aid in continuing our work. In
addition our doors are always open to any and all Town citizens or employees whose comments or suggestions might
be useful.
In Memory of
We note with sorrow the death of our long-term, hard-
working former member Robert H. Walker.
Mr. Robert H. Walker served continuously on the
Personnel Board from 1969 until his death on April 18,
1976. He was Chairman of the Board from 1970 through
1972.
Mr. Walker was a graduate of the University of New
Hampshire and a registered professional engineer. At
the time of his death he was a Major in the Army Re-
serve and was employed as Director of Purchasing and
Personnel at Fitchburg Paper Company.

events and several sub-committees are now functioning--such as, the Parade Committee, Pageant Committee and
the Calendar Committee.
The writing of the history of Lunenburg is being prepared for publication, the result of much research, editing
and typing by a small group of women headed by Nelde K. Drumm and Margaret P. Harley. Dondi Larue, quilting
designer and teacher, has volunteered to oversee the making of a 250th Anniversary quilt whichwill be added to the
Historical Society collection. Noted flag maker, Henry Fredette of Fitchburg, is working on the 250th Flag.
During 1976, the 250th Anniversary Committee took part in the July 3rd Bonfire and related events to pay
tribute to the Nation's Bicentennial. This was done in conjunction with the Fire Department, Jaycees, and other
Town organizations. The reception by the publichas prompted the Committeeto continue this function, at least for
1977, and as part of the 1978 observance.

The 250th Anniversary Committee has been working for about four years to plan a 250th Celebration. With
the assistance of Donald G. Gillis and Mrs. Drumm, date plaques have been prepared for mounting on all houses
built in the 1700's. Granite markers will be placed at locations of prime historical interest, and the granite com-
memorative arch in Lunenburg Center is awaiting a replica of the Town Seal for display.
Now all that is needed are individuals to volunteer for the many tasks, both up-front and behind the scenes,
that will have to be done to make 1978 a truly memorable year. So, if your name is not on a list for a committee,
contact any of the members, and we will find a job for you.

Sewerage Advisory Committee
The 1975 report of the Sewerage Advisory Committee predicted what has turned out to be a highly optimistic
timetable for the Whalom sewerage problem. However, as stated in that report, we have waited until maximum
Federal and State funds are available before going to the Town for a bond issue to cover the Town's share. (The
map of the Phase 1 Program proposed for the Whalom Area is shown on the next page.)
During 1976, maximum Environmental Protection Agency and State grants were committed to the Whalom project
to cover 90% of the eligible costs. If the Town approves a bond issue for this project by September 1, 1977, we can
then go out for bids and construction can proceed.
Preliminary figures indicate that this Whalom project will increase the tax rate by approximately 50 cents to
75 cents per $1000 valuation and will cost each user approximately $10.00 to $12.00 per month, plus a "one-time"
charge to hook-up to the system.
There is a need for sewers in the Whalom Area. Alternatives have been explored and the current proposed
system is deemed to be the best answer. Final cost numbers including a detailed analysis of our financial arrange-
ments with Fitchburg and the East Fitchburg Treatment Plant will be available at a Special Town Meeting.
See you there!

Water Study Committee
At the present time the Water Study Committee does not feel that further studies be undertaken by means of
commissioning a professional engineer. We do feel that a continuing study by Townspeople be done, preferably within
five years. This continuing study should not only monitor the development within the Water District, but should also
study various means of assisting the Water District to provide service to the growth areas of the Town. This study group
should be convened at the discretion of the Selectmen and/or the Planning Board. These conclusions are based upon:
a. The needs of the Water District users appear to be satisfied by the District's plans and proposed programs.
b. The immediate requirements of the individual and mini-district users appear to be satisfied with locally
drilled wells.
C. The report of the Lunenburg Growth and Development Committee indicated a consensus that a slow and
controlled Town growth pattern is desired to maintain a rural atmosphere.
Presently, water is supplied to the people of Lunenburg in two ways:
1. Private systems - these are mostly individual systems supplying one or two dwellings but occasionally up to
as many as twenty dwellings. These last mentioned systems "mini-district" are groups of private homes
attached to a small system such as found at Hickory Hills or Lake Shirley.
2. Water District - the Water District covers approximately 1500 homes or buildings of which approximately
100 are serviced and billed directly from Leominster.
There are in the neighborhood, of 2,690 buildings or dwellings in Lunenburg. These are approximately:2,
single family residence; 119 double family residence; 18 three family residence; 4 four family residence; 41 combined
residence and other; 90 commercial and industrial. Approximately 55% of the homes or buildings are serviced by the
District.
Any capital expenditure involving extension of the District services is dependent upon the majority vote of the
members. It appears it may be quite a while until the District is expanded to satisfy the complete water requirements
of the total Town. Federal and State grants are available for certain expansion projects. However, recent meetings
of the Water District indicate an unwillingness by the users to solicit these grants because of attendant Federal and
State interference in the administration of the grants and the future operation of the District.
The District is presently growing at approximately 12 takers per year and the Town is presently growing at an
average of 32 houses per year. Most of this growth appears to be in areas of the Town not serviced by the District.
The growth pattern indicates that the Water District is gradually servicing a smaller and smaller percentage of the
Town.
The Water District has worked very hard in the past and continues to work to developo systematic appraisal of
the Town's sub-soil water storage condition and to produce fruitful wells. To this end, they have hired professional
help, relied upon various governmental aids and have done extensive sub-soil exploration drilling and testing. It
appears they will develop an adequate supply of water that will meet the standards of the various governmental
health agencies and will also meet the needs of the Water District for the near future.
The Water District has also developed plans for increasing the storage capacity of its aboveground facilities
to maintain adequate pressure and fire facilities for its members. These plans are presently awaiting adequate finan-
cing plans and affirmative vote of the District members.
These plans of the Water District do not appear to meet the need of any industrial growth for the two areas
suggested in Development and Industrial Commission studies -- i.e.:
a. The north end of Town along Chase Road.
b. The south end of Town along Lancaster Avenue and Shirley Road.
-24-
===PAGE 27===
Growth and Development Committee
Chapter 807 of the Acts of 1975 provides for the formulation of a Statewide Growth
and Development Policy based on recommendations, policies and objectives devel-
CONTROL
oped by local Growth and Development Committees. In accordance with the above
Act, Lunenburg's Committee conducted a series of public hearings to solicit the views
of the Townspeople. Based on opinions expressed at these hearings, recommendations by
various Town Boards, and an exhaustive year-long study, our Committee prepared a Local
Growth Statement which emphasizedour Town's ultimate goals. Lunenburg's Statement was submitted to the Regional
Planning Commission together with the statements ofother cities and towns in the Montachusett Region, to be incor-
porated into a Regional Statement.
Our "Future Policy" essentially calls for an effort to become more self-sufficient and to grow only to the
extent that our natural resources will allow US to grow, while still maintaining our present quality of life. Follow-
ing the transmittal of this local policy to the Montachusett Regional Planning Commission (MRPC), MRPC compiled
a draft Growth and Development Statement, which was supposed to reflect a consensus of the opinions of the Reg-
ion's seventeen member committees, and identify planning problems and suggest solutions based on such opinions.
Our Committee feels strongly that the Regional Commission's Statement does not reflect a consensus of the Town
statements, but instead identifies and supports potential solutions to City and Urban area problems, many of which
solutions effectively overlook and even magnify Town or Rural problems.
Lunenburg has protested
against this position, and is urging MRPC to focus on the Rural problems
HELP
in its final report, which has
not yet been issued to the State.
The Regional Report is geared to more governmental aid and assistance, a position which Lunenburg's policy
opposes. This attitude on the part of MRPC, along with certain of their actions in the handling of the entire issue,
led Lunenburg's Growth and Development Committee to begin to question why we are a part of the MRPC and what
it is doing for Lunenburg, either voluntarily or by request. It was found by the Committee that Lunenburg has not
neededor requested any planning assistance from MRPC. Lunenburg didrequest that MRPC seek aid for two projects,
tennis courts and a block grant for use by the Arts and Crafts Association, neither of which requests were granted.
The "Feds" (to guarantee complexity?) also require that paperwork associated with an application for sewer grants be
processed through the Regional Commission.
SOMETHING for NOTHING or NOTHING for SOMETHING?
In its study of the whys and wherefores of our involvement in a regional setup, our Committee learned that
MRPC is primarily funded by government grants (approximately $470,000 last year) and secondarily funded by the
communities ($30,000 last year), which of course means that it is the Federal Government, not the local commun-
ities, which dictates Regional policy, and that in actuality the primary function of MRPC is to channel Federal
Funds into the Region.
The Committee also learned that for the second time in two years abill will be introduced to the Massachu-
setts Legislature (House Bill 1708 "An Act to Organize Regional Government in Massachusetts"), which, if passed,
would eliminate County government and County
taxes, but at the same time establish a new, larger
and more powerful Regional bureaucracy
with regional taxation powers. This in turn would
drastically weaken local government's
powers of self-determination. In view of this po-
tential new law, and the present setup of
the MRPC, our Growthand Development Committee
suggests that the Town of Lunenburg care-
fully re-evaluate its continued relationship with
the Regional Planning Agency. We are
in favor of a Regional Planning Agency which is
a forum at which member
communities can discuss and solve their
mutual problems, but
vehemently opposed
to continued member-
ship in an agency
which is essentially
tool for governmental
bureaucracy, and
is in fact a growing
governmental
bureaucracy itself.
REGIONAL GOV'T IS TOTALITARIAN GOV'T!
-25-

Fire Department
In 1976 the Fire Department answered 376 calls for
assistance as follows: Rescue Unit Calls 190; Service
Calls 34; Mutual Aid 26; Brush Fires 31; Car Fires 22;
Building Fires 14; Needless Calls 11; Chimney Fires 11;
False Alarms 9; Town Dump 7; Rescue 4; Accidents 5;
Boat Fires 2; Cooking Fires 3; Electrical Fires 3; Gas Fire
1; Dryer Fire 1; Accidental Alarms 2. This was the most
active year the Department has ever had, averaging more
than a call per day. The largest increase was in Rescue
Unit calls, going from 136 in 1975 to 190 in 1976.
We are still operating our Rescue Unit with two
squads of eight men each, alternating on calls. By July
of 1977 all our Rescue Unit personnel will be registered
Emergency Medical Technicians.
In 1977 residents will be asked to voteon replace-
ment of our Rescue Unit which is nine years old and is in
need of repair. Since it does not comply with today's
standards, it does not seem practical to repair it.
The new fire truck which was ordered in August of
1975 should be here by the spring of 1977. Also in the
spring of 1977, the new addition to the Fire/Police Sta-
tion should be completed. Our other fire trucks, a 1958
forest fire truck, a 1961 FWD pumper, and a 1966 FWD
James P. Deming, Fire Chief
pumper-tanker are in good condition.
Training sessions were held throughout the year for both rescue personnel and firefighters with Jon Jones, David
Demers, Charles Dyer, Charles Billiter and Henry Kelley acting as instructors. Outside instructors were also used.
I would like to thank the officers and members of the Department for their fine cooperation throughout the year.

Highway Department
Rebirth of the Highways. From the 1830's on, high-
ways took second place to the railroads in the building of
our nation. While city streets were maintained, country
roads were left to deteriorate. Meanwhile the railroads
crossed the continent and reached into every area of the
country - until by 1880 there were more miles of rails than
of surfaced roads.
It was the bicycle, oddly enough, that first focused
attention on the deplorable state of the roads. In 1885,
the low-wheeled "safety bicycle" was introduced and it
touched off a bicycle boom. But the bike riders found the
going extremely rough - and soon they were organizing for
better roads.
The cyclists found themselves some powerful - if
unlikely - allies in the farmers and the railroads. The
farmers began to realize that good roads would give them
the freedom to take their produce to market when the price
was best. And the railroads became convinced that im-
proved roads would extend the area from which they could
draw traffic.
George G. Munyon, Jr., Highway Superintendent
In 1892, the National League for Good Roads was
founded and just a year later the Federal Government re-
sponded by opening the Office of Road Inquiry. The Office was the first federal involvement in road building since
the last appropriation for National Road in 1838.
By the 1880's the technology was there to build better roads. The development of the steam engine brought
the invention of many important labor and money saving devices for the road builders; steam-powered jaw rock crusher
was invented in 1858. Add to that the steam shovel, steam roller, steam mortar-mixer and the development of both
Portland and bituminous cement surfaces. All of these changes in the late nineteenth century set the stage for a re-
birth of the highway. But by 1904 the Office of Road Inquiry reported only 141 miles of roads surfaced with some-
thing better than water-bound macadam (small stones).
Finally, it was the automobile that gave the necessary impetus to road improvement. With the appearance of
the Model T, the auto was no longer a luxury few could afford. The motor truck began to find its place as an im-
portant form of freight transport. With the advent of World War I, it became evident that the nation needed a net-
work of highways to provide relief for the railroads and a viable alternative means of transport for the defense
of the nation. The highway boom had begun. By 1920 there were ten million motor cars on the roads, and a year
later the nation's first national highway system was enabled by the Federal Highway Act. A new era of road con-
struction had begun. (Excerpt from ITT Industrial Credit Co., New England Construction Magazine)
The following is a report of services performed and projects accomplished by the Highway Department on the
Town's 76.97 miles of accepted roads:
Chapter 90 Improvement - A mix-in-place surface was applied to Lancaster Avenue, Leominster Road and Electric
Avenue. A hone job was done on Holman Street and Northfield Road, a base coat and seal was applied to Leominster
Road and a curb laid around the island at the intersection of Leominster Road and Prospect Street. This work cost
$40,965.82 and is completely funded by the State. Under the 1974-1975 contracts, Leominster Road was reconstructed
and drainage installed completing this contract at a cost of $36,310.45.

Rolling Acres Road at a cost of $5,503.60. Various roads were swept, stone dust patching, shoulders of roads were
cut back, guard rail installed on Chase Road and Cross Street, spring cleaning, roadside mowing and brush cut on

Tree Warden
There is a new pesticide, Lignasan, on the market which
is injected into the basal roots or the trunk of a tree for control
of Dutch Elm disease. It has been used with good results at the
Shade Tree Laboratory in Amherst.
During 1976, one hundred and eight diseased trees were
cut, chipped and buried. The State Department of Natural Re-
sources gave five days ($600.00) of tree removal work and two
days work which consisted of tagging forty-two trees suspected
of having Dutch Elm disease. The Town was reimbursed $508.61
by the County and a total of $9,092.33 was spent under the
Dutch Elm program.
Twenty-four trees were purchased and planted on various
streets within the Town under the Tree Warden Department. The
work accomplished under this program cost $5,826.00.
The Tree Warden wishes to remind residents that most pes-
ticides are poisonous. Spray only when pest control is essential.
Richard W. Brown, Tree Warden

Police Department
Police Department: left to right - Patrolman Steven G. Whitney, Patrolman Alphonse J. Baron, Sergeant Roger A.
Fitzgerald, Police Chief Frederick B. Hobbs, Sergeant Hector J. Morin, Patrolman Michael A. Bottalico and
Patrolman Richard L. Lambert, Jr. Not present when picture was taken was Patrolman August N. Misner.
Construction is well underway on the Police Department addition to the Fire Station. Once the work is com-
pleted and the Police Department moves in, an Open House will be held so that Townspeople can view the new
quarters.
In 1976, 5,976 calls for service were received by the Lunenburg Police Department. This averages out to more
than 16 calls a day that require action by a police officer, but does not include the sundry amount of calls handled
that are of a routine nature. Some of the more serious crimes committed in Lunenburg included 117 breaks into build-
ings; attempted breaks or breaking, entering and larceny; 164 thefts, 72 of property valued at over $100 and 92
of property valued at under $100; 3 armed robberies; 1 unarmed robbery; 41 assaults including 3 by means of a
dangerous weapon and 1 on a person under 14; 27 stolen motor vehicles; 34 hit and run accidents; and 124 acts of
vandalism.
Other activities of the Police Department included 1063 house checks; serving of 399 subpoenas; and respond-
ing to 404 alarms from private homes and businesses. There were 611 citations issued as a result of stopping 1335
motor vehicles for various violations and the setting up of 256 radar traps. Also, 302 motor vehicle accidents were
investigated and 48 people were taken into protective custody. A total of 162 gun permits of various types were
issued.
During the past year, Police Department personnel started making modifications and improvements in the records
keeping system. These changes will aid in compiling data necessary to meet the new requirement of the Department
of Public Safety to report certain crime statistics on a weekly, monthly and annual basis. One of the requirements
of the Police Department commencing in 1977 will be the Massachusetts First Responder Training Law and the Cardio-
pulmonary Resuscitation Law requiring extensive first-aid training. Legislation now requires a twelve week training
program for newly appointed police officers within one year of their appointment. Police departments must have
officers especially trained to investigate rape or othersex related crimes; to operate a breathalyzer machine;conduct
drug investigations; finger printing; and photography.
-30-
===PAGE 33===
It is becoming more difficult for small town departments to keep abreast of all of these requirements and to
continue to meet increased demands for service, and to provide adequate protection for the residents of the Town of
Lunenburg.

Inspector of Plumbing & Gas Fitting
During 1976, a total 132 Plumbing and Gas Permits
were issued. Twenty-five Plumbing Permits were issued for
new homes, 36 for remodeled homes and 9 for commercial
buildings. There were 9 Gas Permits for new homes, 44
for various gas appliance replacements and 9 for commer-
cial buildings.
In case of an emergency, a gas appliance may be
turned on temporarily by the Licensed Installer or the serv-
ing Gas Supplier, provided, the Installer has tested the
piping in accordance with the appropriate Section of the
Gas Code; the Installer and the Supplier are satisfied that
the installation of piping and use of the equipment will
assure safe operation; and the Gas Inspector is notified
and regular inspection is made at the earliest opportunity
but in any case not later than the next working day. In
case of fire or explosion involving facilities regulated by
the Gas Board, the Gas Inspector shall promptly notify
the Gas Regulatory Board in reasonable detail and shall
impound any gas piping or gas equipment involved which
in his opinion contributed to the explosion or fire.
Thomas E. Mortis
No person shall engage in the business of a Master
Plumber or work as a Journeyman or Apprentice, nor solicit, by sign, listing or any other form of advertisement, work
regulated or controlled by the Plumbing Board, or by any ordinance, by-law, rule or regulation made hereunder,
unlesshe is lawfully registered, or hasbeen licensed by the Examiners as provided by the Plumbing Code. Any person
so licensed as a Master Plumber or Journeyman may carry on the work of a gas fitter throughout the Commonwealth,
notwithstanding any local ordinance, by-law, rule or regulation to the contrary, and may engage in the work of in-
stalling house drainage and connecting with common sewers without being required to have any local license therefor,
but shall be subject to local regulations relative to permits and bonding requirements.
Inspector of Weights & Measures
Lunenburg residents have the right to expect that
whatever they buy is measured or weighed accurately--
to assure this accuracy is the responsibility of the Inspector
of Weights and Measures.
Scales, gasoline pumps, fuel oil truckmeters, other
commercially used weighing/measuring devices are reg-
ularly tested for accuracy once a year and unannounced
spot checks are frequently made. Of the weighing and
measuring devices inspected in 1976, 60 were found to be
correct and sealed, 10 scales and pumps were adjusted,
and two pumps condemned.

Laws, all inhabitants or persons having usual places of

Building Inspector
The Tri-Town Building Inspector Board has been in effect since February of 1975 under the requirements of
M.G.L.A. Chapter 802, Acts of 1972. In this time, I found that many residents in our Town were not aware of the
new State Building Code and the services provided with respect to public safety and safety within private homes or
in any form of structure.
The Tri-Town Building Inspector Board has exempted all municipal buildings and buildings used for religious pur-
poses from permit fees. However, these buildings are required by law to have building permits and the regular re-
quired inspections.
The Building Inspector's office is open five days a week from 8:00 A.M. to 4:00 P.M. and is located in the
Memorial Building on Memorial Drive (Telephone: 582-4613). Miss Donna Wright, Secretary, is available to assist
residents when the Building Inspector is not in the office. Applications and information relative to building permits
may also be obtained at and completed applications delivered to the Selectmen's Office, Town Hall.
I wish to take this opportunity to express my appreciation to residents and municipal officials for their coopera-
tion and assistance.
The following is an excerpt from the Massachusetts State Building Code: Section 124.1 Duties of Building Of-
ficial - Unsafe Buildings: The building official, immediately upon being informed by report or otherwise that abuild-
ing or other structure or anything attached thereto or connected therewith is dangerous to life or limb or that any
building in that city or town is unused, uninhabited or abandoned, and open to weather, shall inspect the same; and
he shall forthwith in writing notify the owner as provided in Section 122.12, as defined in Article 2, to remove it or
make it safe if it appears to him to be dangerous, or to make it secure if it is unused, uninhabited or abandoned and
open to weather. If it appears that such structure would be especially unsafe in case of fire, it shall be deemed dan-
gerous within the meaning hereof, and the building official may affix in a conspicuous place upon its exterior walls
a notice of its dangerous condition, which shall not be removed or defaced without authority from him.
During the period from January 1, 1976 through December 31, 1976, $3,717.50 was received by the Town for
building permits. A total of 194 applications for permits were submitted and 189 permits were issued as follows:

Veterans' Services
Due to unforeseen circumstances, the expenditures of
this Department exceeded the appropriated funds by ap-
proximately 25%. This was primarily due to a 5.5% cost-
of-living increase granted by the State, labor disputes and
the ever-increasing rates of hospitals, nursing homes, doc-
tors, prescription drugs, fuel and other necessities of life.
This Department had at least seventeen permanent
cases per month and two to four temporary assistance cases
due to the increased unemployment in this area, and per-
sons over sixty-five years of age who were eligible for as-
sistance were also aided. Looking into the future we find
that the veterans of World War II were some of the new re-
cipients applying for assistance, and these World War II
veterans will soon be at retirement age.
At the start of 1976 the monthly expenses for bene-
fits were approx imately $3,500.00, but this figure increased
to approximately $5,500.00 during the winter season due
to recipients being given an increase in Shelter Allowance
which had not previously been taken into account.
I wish to take this opportunity to thank the Town Hall
staff for their assistance and the Town Officials for their
Alfred H. Gauthier, Veterans' Agent
trust in me.
Dog Officer
Approximately 1,340 calls were recorded during 1976 with most of the calls concerning lost dogs. This was a
very encouraging year with regard to the issuance of dog licenses aided by a letter program instituted with the help
of the Town Clerk's Office. This letter program will be instituted again in 1977 and should aid in the licensing pro-
gram. I have found in most cases of dog problems, that if they are dealt with on an individual basis with follow-up
measures taken, neighbors--in a spirit of cooperation--resolve many of the problems.
At this time I wish to commend the efforts of the residents in close proximity to the Elementary Schools who
have kept the problem dogs confined during school hours so as to minimize problems occurring at the schools during
recess hours and at bus times. With the confinement of these problem animals, injuries to children while on Town
property were kept to a minimum. Our most troublesome area is still in the Whalom where there remains a large
number of dogs running at large during the daylight hours. These animals congregate at the school bus pick-upareas
and after the pick-ups are made these animals run in packs harassing residents. An individual effort is needed by
residents of the Whalom Area to keep a close check on their dogs. With the onset of winter and the heavy snow cover,
our streets are hazardous enough without packs of dogs running in and out of traffic.
Since the enactment of the by-law concerning confinement of unspayed female dogs during the breeding season,
there has been a significant decrease in the number of packs of dogs in our more heavily populated areas. Our most
difficult problemnow at hand is with dogs molesting livestock and fowl--most often resulting in money losses to owners
of the dogs and often the death of the animals due to the nature of the injuries inflicted. In all but one case, dog
owners were notified and the problems resolved by mutual agreement.
I would like at this time to thank the following departments for their assistance during my recent illness: the
Highway Department, Police Department, Fire Department, Dispatchers and the Town Clerk and her staff. With their
assistance, this department did not have a lapse in service to Town residents.
-34-
===PAGE 37===
Extension Service
The Lunenburg 4-H Clubs have had a busy year. At present there are 5 active clubs: Crafts (small animals
and sewing) led by Dot Connell; Cooking with Agnes Price; Outdoor Adventure started in September by Joyce Downey;
Crafts led by Ruth Anderson; and Bridle Path Horse Club with new leaders Dixie Beck and Judy Smith who took over
for Diane Moore Tanguay. Individual club work was held in January. February brought a Pot Luck Supper and 4-H
demonstrations with over eighty attending. Winners went on to County competition in March.
New Town Committee officers were elected in March: Town Chairman, Judith Smith; Secretary, Dixie Beck;
Treasurer, Elizabeth Hollis along with the following Trustees: Richard Bishop, Edward Stillmanand Raymond Proctor.
During Easter week in April, all the 4-H Clubs held their bus tripto Sturbridge Village sponsored by the Town Com-
mittee. Also in April leaders and members attended the State Leaders' Field Day held in Grinnell Arena at the Univ-
ersity of Massachusetts. April saw the Bridle Path Horse Club collecting pledges and Iriding in the Easter Seals Ride-
a-thon.
May twenty-third brought Achievement Day during which awards for the year were given out, a Bridle Path
Club Horse Show was held along with cook-out for all attending members and parents. In June the Bridle Path Club
sponsored their annual 4-H Horse Show held at the Leominster Rod and Gun Club featuring 28 English and Western
Classes. June also brought atrip to Pennsylvania under the exchange program for two members of Dot Connells' club.
Also eight 4-H members attended the University of Massachusetts State Conference the week of June 24th.
Several leaders helpedwith a Bake Sale during the July 4th Bicentennial Weekend which was heldon the High
School grounds. Summer found eleven 4-H members attending Horse Camp in Spencer and 2going to Camp Marshall
sponsored by the Town Committee and the Bridle Path Horse Club. Several Horse Club members worked as teaching
counselors at Camp Spencer for the summer. This year at the Grange Fair, there was a 4-H Booth showing Club Ac-
tivities a display of riding equipment; show ribbons and trophies; a cooking demonstration given by members of
Agnes Price's Club showing how to make ice cream. The Bridle Path Club also held a gymkhana for local children
owning horses. Games were played for prizes and ribbons. Dot Connell and Ruth Anderson were Youth Chairmen for
exhibits at the Fair.
A new club "Outdoor Adventure" for teenagers was organized by Joyce Downey and members have been busy
planning mountain climbing trips, making backpacks and getting ready for outdoor activities this spring and summer.
Two craft leaders, Dot Connell and Ruth Anderson, traveled to Clinton to an area leaders' meeting where they gave
a demonstration on dried flower arrangements. Ruth's club has been busy making Christmas, tree decorations, stuffed
animals, pillows and various other crafts.
October saw Bridle Path members participating in the Homecoming Parade; holding a Pancake Breakfast; and
a mounted meeting with horseback riding lessons. All clubs
took part in the October Cookie Sale. October was en-
rollment night for all 4-H Clubs featuring a Pot Luck Sup-
per for all prospective members and their families in addi-
tion to discussion of the varied programs offered. Octo-
ber 30th was the Bridle Path Club Horse Show with over
130 horses entered in the competition. This year, awards
received were Donna Camber, Key Award, Horse Project
Award and top Worcester County Horse Member; and Patty
Cutting, Key Award and Citizenship Award and she was
also selected as 4th Alternate for the Eastern States 4-H
Horse Show representing Worcester County.
On November 11th Lunenburg 4-H members were
guests on radio station WEIM in Fitchburg for a talk show
explaining what 4-H Club is and why it is important. Also
a trip was made to a horse rest home in November.

ARTICLE XIX
REGULATIONS OF CERTAIN ACTIVITIES ON WHALOM LAKE
Section 1. Definitions. The following words as used in this By-law unless a different meaning is required by the context, or as
specifically prescribed, shall have the following meanings:
(a) "Vehicle(s)" shall include all motor vehicles both registered and unregistered including but not limited to snow vehicles,
recreational vehicles, motorcycles etc.
(b) "Public Access" shall mean the public access located in the City of Leominster.
(c) "Law Enforcement Officer" shall mean any police officer of the Town of Lunenburg or City of Leominster, fish and game
wardens, members of the State Police and employees of the Commonwealth having police powers on public lands.
Section 2. No person, group of persons or organizations shall operate, or cause to be operated, any vehicle on Whalom Lake without
a written Permit from the Board of Selectmen of the Town of Lunenburg or other Municipal or State Agency duly authorized by law.
Section 3. Every application for a Permit made by an individual shall be signed by the applicant therefor, who shall give his or her
full name and home address.
Section 4. Every application for a Permit made by a partnershipshall state the full names and home addresses of all the members of
the partnership and shall be signed by a majority thereof.
Section 5. Every application for a Permit made by an association shall signed by a majority of the members of the governing body
thereof, who shall state their full names and home addresses.
Section 6. Every applicant for a Permit required by the provisions of Section 5, Chapter 110, General Laws, to file certificate
stating the real name of a person conducting a business, shall file with his application a certified copy thereof.
Section 7. Every application for a Permit made by a corporation shall state the full names and home addresses of the president,
treasurer, clerk and secretary, directors and manager or other principal representative of the corporation. It shall be signed by some
officer duly authorized by a vote of its board of directors or other similar board. A copy of such vote certified by the clerk or sec-
retary of the corporation, together with a copy of the certificate of its organization, shall accompany the application. A copy of
the vote appointing its manager or other principal representative shall also accompany the application.
Section 8. All applications for Permits shall be made upon blanks furnished by the said Board of Selectmen and shall be fully answered
in detail and shall be typewritten or legibly written in ink. Applications written in pencil, in whole or in part, will not be accepted.
Any false statement contained in any application shall be a cause or ground for refusing to grant the Permit or for suspending, can-
celling or revoking a Permit already granted.
Section 9. As many police officers shall be assigned to duty at Whalom Lake as may reasonably be deemed necessary by the Chief
or Senior officer in charge of the Police Department of the Town of Lunenburg in order to properly and effectively police any activity
thereon which will involve the operation of vehicles, whenever in the judgment of the Chief or such Senior officer, there is a like-
lihood that there will be a substantial number of participants in, and/or persons present to witness the activity. The expense incurred
in providing such officers shall be borne by the applicant for the Permit.
Section 10. No person shall operate a vehicle on Whalom Lake who has not attained the age of sixteen and one-half (16½) years.
Section 11. No vehicle shall be operated on Whalom Lake within five hundred (500) feet of a residence, or in a negligent manner
so that the lives and safety of the public might be endangered or the property of others might be damaged.
Section 12. It shall be unlawful to possess or keep in and upon a vehicle on Whalom Lake any alcoholic beverage or any controlled
substance as defined by General Laws, Chapter 94C, as amended.
Section 13. No vehicle or any part thereof, shall be allowed to remain on Whalom Lake later than sunset on any day and, in default
thereof, shall be subject to being towed or otherwise removed and impounded at the expense of the owner.
Section 14. It shall be unlawful for any person to discharge in or upon Whalom Lake any pollutant or to discard therein or thereupon,
litter of any kind or upon the ways adjacent thereto, including Lakefront Road, Prospect Street, Lakeside Avenue and in and about
the vicinity of the public access area. Whoever violates any part of this section shall be punished by a fine of not less than One
Hundred Dollars ($100.00) nor more than Three Hundred Dollars ($300.00).
Section 15. No vehicle shall enter upon Whalom Lake except by the public access.
Section 16. The Permit of any holder thereof who, in the conduct of the activity permitted, commits or permitsany violation or in-
fraction of any part of this By-law shall be subject to modification, suspension, cancellation or revocation.
Section 17. Whoever violates any part of Sections 2, 11, 12 and 15 hereof, shall be punished by a fine of not less than Twenty-
Five Dollars ($25.00) nor more than One Hundred Dollars ($100.00).

===PAGE 42===
Lunenburg has combined the offices of the Town Clerk, Tax Collector and Treasurer since 1916, with one full-
time person serving in the three capacities. Throughout the years, this combination has proven to be an excellent
way of overcoming the inherent weaknesses in the stereotype of small town government, wherein each office is held
by a part-time, often unavailable official. Our Town Office has been able to overlap its various functions, and
handle all sorts of Town business without referring people from here to there and back to get answers or assistance.
Not only does this system permit US to serve the
public more competently, but also to use personnel more
effectively, by channeling their energies in different di-
rections as peak workloads occur in one or another of the
three "departments".
Town Clerk
Since the Town Clerk serves as a member of the
Boardof Registrars and oversees elections and Town Meet-
ings, and since the Town Clerk's office has many responsi-
bilities in connection with elections, in a year like 1976
in which a presidential election occurs, Town Office per-
sonnel are kept busy registering voters, processing absentee
ballots, preparing voting lists, and setting up for the var-
ious primaries and elections, which duties are complicated
by the necessity of keeping up with the constantly chang-
ing election laws. Following are excerpts from the Pres-
idential Primary, Annual Town Meeting, State Primary,
Posey Carey issues sporting license-
State Election, and Special Town Meeting Minutes.
a popular Town Office commodity

ARTICLE 4. To see if the Town will vote to amend Section 24 of Article XV of the Town's By-laws entitled "Salary Adminis-
tration Plan of the Town of Lunenburg" by deleting in the first and second clauses thereof the words "10¢ per mile" and substituting
therefor the words "12¢ per mile", and by deleting the words "$5.00 per trip" and substituting therefor the words "$6.00 per trip",
so that the section then reads: "Travel reimbursement shall be by signed voucherat the rate of 12c permile when using the employee's
own car, plus and parking fees, provided, however, that based on the 12¢ per mile figure, Police Officers providing their own
transportation to Superior Court in Worcestershall be reimbursed in the amount of $6.00 per trip.", or act anything thereon. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY. (Approved by the Finance Committee as voted.)
