ARTICLE 6. To see if the Town will vote to amend its By-laws by adding to Article II thereof, the following new sections to
be known respectively as Section 12 and Section 13, as follows: "Section 12. So long as the Town of Lunenburg continues to share
the services and compensation of the Building Inspector with one or more towns, the Board of Selectmen shall annually fix the salary
or compensation of the Building Inspector as soon as may be after the passage of the annual budget, and may determine, in addition
-42-
===PAGE 45===
thereto, the kind and range of fringe benefits, so-called, which shall attach to the position of Building Inspector, provided, however,
the kind and range of such fringe benefits shall not exceed those afforded other town employees under the provisions of the Salary
Administration Plan of the Town, Section 13. So long as the Town of Lunenburg continues to share the services and compensation of
the Building Inspector with one or more towns, the Board of Selectmen may, upon such terms as the Board deems appropriate, enter
into an Agreement with such other town or towns for the purpose of arranging to have such other town or towns turn over to the Town
of Lunenburg its respective share of the salary or compensation of the Building Inspector. Such funds shall be kept in a separate ac-
count by the Treasurer of the Town of Lunenburg. Thereafter, at such time as the Building Inspector is entitled to receive the com-
pensation due him from the Town of Lunenburg in the usual and regular course of business, the Treasurershall include in a single pay-
ment the amounts due from all towns for the period for which payment is being made and the separate account containing the funds
of such other town or towns shall be charged for their proportionate share of the compensation or salary of the Building Inspector.
Said proportionate shares shall be included in and properly identified in the payroll voucher.", or act anything thereon. VOTED
UNANIMOUSLY.

QUESTION NO. 1 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on August 15, 1973, by a vote of 261-0, and on May 14, 1975,
by a vote of 217-55?
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would provide that equality under the law may not be denied or abridged on the basis
of sex, race, color, creed or national origin. This amendment adds one sentence to Article 1 of Part the First of the Constitution
which now contains a general statement of individual rights, including the right to enjoy and defend life and liberty and the right
to acquire and protect property.
VOTE:
YES - 2585
NO - 1190
BLANKS 166
-50-
===PAGE 53===
QUESTION NO. 2 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on August 15, 1973, by a vote of 199-66, and on May 7, 1975,
by a vote of 228-41
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would authorize the Legislature to substitute for the present system of flat or uniform
personal income tax rates a system of rates graduated according to the total amount of income received. The Legislature would also
be authorized to provide for reasonable exemptions, deductions, credits, and abatements and could base Massachusetts income tax
provisions on provisions of Federal income tax law.
VOTE:
YES - 854
NO - 2904
BLANKS - 183
QUESTION NO. 3 - PROPOSED AMENDMENT TO THE CONSTITUTION
Do you approve of the adoption of an amendment to the Constitution summarized below, which was approved by the General
Court in joint sessions of the House of Representatives and Senate on August 15, 1973 by a vote of 259-0, and on May 12, 1976, by
a vote of 262-1?
SUMMARY - The proposed amendment would authorize the Legislature to provide for absentee voting by persons who hold
religious beliefs in conflict with the act of voting on the day on which any election is to be held.
VOTE:
YES - 2135
NO - 1572
BLANKS - 234
QUESTION NO. 4 - LAW PROPOSED BY AN INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 5, 1975, by a
vote of 179-46, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 7, 1975?
SUMMARY - SECTION 1 of the act inserts a new chapter 164B into the General Laws and establishes a Massachusetts Power
Authority, a body corporate and politic with seven members appointed by the Governor to staggered six year terms. The Authority
is to establish and operate a bulk power supply system to supply wholesale electric power to utilities throughout the Commonwealth.
The primary purpose of the Authority is to supply the Commonwealth with powerwith the minimum adverse impact on the environment.
The Authority isalso authorized to engage in research and development of new sources of power, new siting techniques, and methods
of environmental protection.
In carrying out its responsibilities, the Authority is authorized to adopt by-laws; adopt an official seal; maintain offices; sue
and be sued; construct or acquire facilities either within or without the Commonwealth; issue revenue bonds and borrow money in
anticipation of issuance of revenue bonds; acquire real and personal property; employ professional, managerial and other employees
deemed necessary and fix their compensation to be paid solely out of revenues of the Authority; appear before other government
agencies; apply for and receive federal or other grants of funds; and enter into contracts and agreements.
The Authority will buildand operate all new generatingar transmission facilities in the Commonwealth and has the option to
urchase existing facilities through negotiation, condemnation, or eminent domain. After an initial two-year period, no other utility
may construct a new facility unless the Authority certifies that if lacks, the capability to finance the facility and the facility would
further the purposes of the act.
The Authority will finance its activities by issuing revenue bonds. The bonds will be exempt from state taxation, but will not
be backed by the full faith and credit of the Commonwealth. Power will besold to other utilitiesby contract but no special discounts
or bonuses to promote the increased use of power may be given. Public hearings are required on all major contracts.
The Authority is required to develop a master, 20-year demand study and siting plan within 18 months of its incorporation,
to be updated each succeeding year. Sites will be selected in accordance with the Electric Power Facilities Siting Council Act of
1973. The Governor and the community in which any facility is to be located must affirmatively approve the facilitybefore it can
be constructed.
The Authority will be subject to all applicable federal and state environmental standards and must obtainall necessary federal
and state permits and complete all necessary environmental impact statements.
The Authority will be exempt from taxation but will make payments in lieu of taxes to cities and towns in an amount equal to
the tax which would be paid if the Authority's real and personal property were owned by a private electric utility company.
The Authority is forbidden from engaging in promotional or image advertising. The Authority has the authority to bargain
collectively with its employees and is subject to the provisions of Chapter 150 of the General Laws, which governs the conciliation
and arbitration of industrial disputes. Employees of the Authority are not subject to the civil service law and rules. Employees of
utilities displaced by the activities of the Authority have first préference in employment by the Authority.
SECTION 2 of the act amends section 43 of Chapter 164 of the General Laws to provide that if a city or town votes, in ac-
cordance with the provision of Chapter 164, to establish a municipal utility and acquire the facilities of the utility currently serving
the community, and the utility refuses to sell its property to the city or town, that the Department of Public Utilities will establish
a fair price for the facilities, and the utility will be required to accept the price determined by the department and tender the deed
for the facilities to the city or town.
VOTE:
YES - 520
NO - 3241
BLANKS - 180
-51-
===PAGE 54===
QUESTION NO. 5 - LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 3, 1976, by a
vote of 197-35, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 5, 1976?
SUMMARY - The proposed legislation would prohibit the possession, ownership, or sale of any weapon from which a shot or
bullet can be discharged andwhich has a barrel length of less than sixteen inches. The prohibition would not apply to military per-
sonnel, law enforcement officers, federally licensed handgun manufacturers and wholesalers, common carriers in the ordinary course
of transport, or to historical societies and museums. The act would not affect the possession of rifles, shotguns, and certain antiques
and replicas. The proposal also does not change the existing statutory penalties for unlawful possession, ownership or sale of hand-
guns, including provision imposing mandatory jail sentences.
The proposal would permit owners of handguns to surrender their weapons to any law enforcement agency in the Commonwealth
within six months of the effective date of the act without incurring criminal liability. Those surrendering handguns within that six
months will be compensated at a rate to be determined by the Commissioner of Public Safety.
VOTE:
YES - 845
NO - 2976
BLANKS - 120
QUESTION NO. 6 - LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 3, 1976, by a
vote of 146-85, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 5, 1976?
SUMMARY - The proposed act would require every beverage container sold or offered for sale in the Commonwealth to have
a refund value of at least five (5) cents, and would prohibit the sale of metal beverage containers with flip-tops. It would apply to
containers for beer and other malt beverages and to soft drinks. It would not apply to containers for dairy products or natural fruit
juices, nor to containers which are biodegradable.
The act would authorize the Secretary of Environmental Affairs to certify containers as reusable or recyclable. It contains
both enforcement and penalty provisions and would take effect on February 1, 1977.
VOTE:
YES - 1967
NO - 1903
BLANKS - 71
QUESTION NO. 7 - LAW PROPOSED BY INITIATIVE PETITION
Do you approve of a law summarized below, which was disapproved by the House of Representatives on May 3, 1976, by a
vote of 182-49, and on which no vote was taken by the Senate before May 5, 1976?
SUMMARY - The proposed would impose a general requirement that every electric utility company charge a uniform rate
per kilowatt hour of electricity. The proposed act would except from this general rule rates charged to other electric utility com-
panies and to residential customers whoheat their principle place of residence by electricity. The Act would also permit a different
rate to be charged residential customers for the first three hundred (300) kilowatt hours they consume each month, andwould authorize
"peak load" pricing whereby a higher rate than the uniform rate per kilowatt hour may be charged during the periods of the day or
seasons of the year when consumption of electricity is the greatest. The Act would authorize the Department of Public Utilities to
issue implementing rules and regulations and provides for enforcement.
VOTE:
YES - 666
NO - 3150
BLANKS - 125
QUESTION NO. 8 - THIS QUESTION IS NOT BINDING
The following is a non-binding advisory question: "Shall the General Court enact legislation authorizing the construction of
an oil refinery and a deep water port, subject to the approval of those communities directly affected and any reservations that the
General Court may prescribe?
SUMMARY - The Legislature has placed this question on the ballot in order to determine whether the people favor or oppose
the construction of an oil refinery and deep water port in Massachusetts. The vote on this question is not binding the Legislature.
The question deals with the general advisability of such construction and is not a specific proposal for a facility. If a specific pro-
posal is made, it would be subject to approval by the communities directly affected and subject to any restrictions imposed by the
Legislature.
VOTE:
YES - 2813
NO - 900
BLANKS - 228
QUESTION NO. 9 - THIS QUESTION IS NOT BINDING
The following is a non-binding advisory question: "Shall retail stores including package liquor stores, so-called, be allowed
to open for business on Sunday ?"
SUMMARY - The Legislature has placed this question on the ballot in order to determine whether the people favor or oppose
the Sunday opening of certain retail stores, including package liquor stores. As the law now stands, most retail and all package
liquor stores must be closed on Sundays. The vote on this question is not binding on the Legislature.
VOTE:
YES - 2209
NO - 1550
BLANKS - 182

In 1976, 792 new voters were registered. Of the 3,941 votes cast at the Presidential Election in November,
143 were by absentee ballot. Absentee ballots are processed to servicemen and their families, any other persons who
will be out of Town on the day of the election, and persons who are disabled and unable to go to the polls in person.
As the time involved in processing these ballots is disproportionate, it is too bad that the procedure cannot be simp-
lified in the interest of saving time and in the interest of the voter himself, who often finds the process too compli-
cated to bother with.

===PAGE 56===
By far the most unsettling event involving elections and Town Meet-
ings was the retirement after the Annual Town Meeting of our extremely
capable Moderator, Calvin K. Sholl, who had served in that capacity since
1970. Cal's knowledge of parliamentary procedure, his communicative
skills, and his command of the podium, all combined with his ease of man-
ner and sense of humor, contributed much to the smooth transaction of the
Town's business on Town Meeting Day. Cal has set quite a precedent, and
it will take some doing to follow his act. Fortunately, Howard Brock, a
veteran Town Official with exceptional interest in the Town's welfare, was
elected as Cal's successor.
During the year, the usual number of Town records, contracts and
agreements were taken into custody, oaths were administered, licenses were
issued, By-laws were processed, mortgages and pole locations were record-
ed, genealogy searches were made, subdivision plans and documents were
dealt with, Appeals Board petitions and decisions were filed, and vital re-
cords were processed and recorded. The following breakdown of vital sta-
tistics highlights the fact that the birth rate in Lunenburg is still dropping,
although it is assumed that this year's 58 is abnormally low as compared to
the average for this time frame, as births for the previous 5 years ranged
Calvin K. Sholl
from 71 to 85 and averaged 80.
Moderator Extraordinaire

24
1977
1976
1977
Writing payrolls, accounting for taxes and other receipts, answering correspondence, duplicating material related to
budgets, Town Meetings, reports, by-laws - only a few of the day to day processes taking place in the Town Office
Tax Collector
Approximately 20,000 tax bills and notices were mailed and processed during Fiscal 1976. Of $2,450,637
in Real Estate Taxes committed, $66,670 was abated, $17,476 was added to Tax Title, $11,786 in Railroad taxes
was placed in Litigation, $4,315 was refunded, and payments of $2,274,316 were received, leaving a balance of
$84,704, or 3 1/2%, uncollected as of June 30, 1976. This is the same as the corresponding percentage uncollected
as of the end of the previous Fiscal Year. There were no prior year Real Estate or Personal Property taxes outstanding
as of the end of Fiscal 1976.
Motor Vehicle Excise receipts totalled $324,711. Although notices mailed to residents who have Fitchburg
mailing addresses, warning them against paying their Motor Vehicle Excise to the City of Fitchburg, have proven ef-
fective in that a considerable number of Fitchburg bills have been forwarded to the Lunenburg Board of Assessors for
reprocessing, Fitchburg is still billing and receiving some portion of Lunenburg's Motor Vehicle Excise revenues. This
is because the State carelessly sends the bills to the city or town shown on the mailing address, as opposed to the actual
place of principal garaging. Whalom residents who have not already done so are urged to return excise bills received
from the City of Fitchburg and request that they be reprocessed to Lunenburg.
On the following page is a full report of all taxes committed and collected during the Fiscal Year 1976:

Treasurer
As government at all levels becomes more and more entwined in red tape, and as municipalities more and
more are trapped into seeking Federal "aid" to offset local spending, the Treasurer's office becomes more and more
inundated in the inevitable reports, questionnaires, etc. which are the hallmarks of big government. One suddenly
realizes that these reports, some of which
are extremely lengthy and complex, have
assumed almost as much importance and
consume almost as much time as the func-
tions for which the offices of Town Treas-
urer and ccounting Officer were created,
namely to receive, disburse and take charge
of all monies belonging to the Town, and
to accurately andprecisely account for the
same.
That is not to minimize the volume
of these basic functions, however, for dur-
ing Fiscal 1976, $11,427,219 was received
by the Town of Lunenburg, and $11, 419, 839
disbursed. As of June 30, 1976, the end of
the Fiscal Year, General and Revenue Shar-
ing cash in the Treasury totalled $45,068,
supplemented by $1,288,473 in invested
funds. In spite of diminishing interest rates
during this 12 month period, income earned
Town Treasurer Mary Padula and assistant Lynn
on invested funds amounted to $70,630.
Markham attack mountains of financial reports
Presented below in considerable detail are the financial reports of the Town for the Fiscal Year 1976.

KEY TO CLASSIFICATION OF ACCOUNTS
AR - Assessors' Recapitulation
FG - Federal Grant
RF - Reserve Fund Transfer
Fwd - Forward
RS - Received from State
B - Borrow
DR - Dog Refund
Int - Interest Earned
TF - Trust Funds
ED - Excess & Deficiency Account
R - Revenue Sharing
TR - Transfer to or from Other Accounts
Rcts - Receipts
It has been 2 1/2 years since we changed from a "Calendar" to a "Fiscal" Financial Year. The primary pur-
pose of this change, which was legislated by the State, was to preclude the necessity of borrowing large sums in
anticipation of revenue. For this to be accomplished, the State adopted a special 18 month (January 1973 through
June 1974) accounting procedure, which allowed cities and towns to build up their surplus funds and to maintain a
higher level of surplus than was maintained under the Calendar Year accounting system. Many cities and towns, fail-
ing to understand how the new system was supposed to work, used up all their surpluses, andare now back to borrowing.
As can be seen on the Balance Sheet, Surplus Revenue in our Town totalled $619,398 as of June 30, 1976.
This is the result of judicious use of our surplus funds, the availability of which removed the necessity of borrowing
and permitted investments. Interest saved on monies not borrowed and earned on funds invested during the last year
alone will have the effect of offsetting over $100,000 worth of expenditures in the coming year. Preliminary calcula-
tions with regard to next year's budget point to a substantial increase in the tax rate. It is hoped that our Town ad-
ministrators will fight off the temptation to drastically reduce our surplus in an effort to ward off such an increase, for
this action would mean that from this time on our Town would be forced into borrowing, and in the long run this can
only result in greater increases in the tax rates of future years.
I have made a careful examination of the books of the Tax Collector
and Town Treasurer, and find the same to be correct and in good order.
(s) James R. Bascom, Auditor

Montachusett Regional Vocational
Technical School District
The size of "Monty Tech" and its ability to enroll those students who have shown an interest in coming to our
school still remains a problem. As a result of the increased numbers of applications, the Administration and School
Committee have been attempting to develop an equitable student enrollment process. The building utilization has
maximized in order to provide Vocational Education to as many as possible, hence, the budget has provided for an
Afternoon Program which has been designed for students from the sending district who are not enrolled in this school
to develop career skills. The emphasis has been changed from exploratory activities to job and career preparation.
The Evening Program has accommodated approximately 2,000 adults. Our day-school program numbered
993 High School students, 16 post-graduates in Trade and Industrial programs, 144 Allied Health post-graduates, and
27 Early Child Care toddlers.
Since our waiting lists have been increasing yearly, advantage has been taken of an opportunity for a project
proposal under the Economic Development Administration for an expansion of the school. While the opportunity, at
this writing, is still present, the probability of approval is less than 10% of the 800 E.D.A. applications. Neverthe-
less, the School Committee and Administration are taking every possible advantage they can in applying for these funds.
Our Craft Advisory Committees have been meeting on a regular basis, thus having a direct interrelationship with
local industry and the specific programs at the school. They are providing input into course content, recommenda-
tions for equipment, cooperative study stations and, thus, act as additional quality control for the programs being
taught.
As in the past, graduation exercises have grown in participation and, hence, three graduations to accommodate
friends and relatives were held for the High School graduates, Allied Health Careers and Post-Graduate programs.
Of the 353 total graduates, over 90% either received employment in related fields, went on to higher education, or
joined the service.
The special orientation for new students was designed with student/parent counseling after aptitude testing and
two days spent with incoming students prior to the start of regular classes. Career objectives, course requirements,
school regulations, opportunities within the school, and ancillary services were discussed with the students.
Teacher workshops were designed to cope with the many changes and regulations as a result of legislation at
both the Federal and State levels. The tenets of Title IX, Chapter 622 and Chapter 766 created the need for revi-
sions of materials, course content and classroom activities. The staff discussed and developed the materials and have
been continually implementing changes in content and format. An Annual Open House washeld on March 7, 1976,
with many additional group visitations by civic and fraternal organizations who were desirous of learning more about
our school.
This past year, as a result of reimbursements, it was possible to reduce our assessments to the cities and towns
by $2,309,796. These reimbursements reduced the budget from $4,190,090 to actual assessments of $1,880,294.
Though there are still unmet needs by the school and additional legislative requisites, the Administration is cogni-
zant of the ever-increasing costs by an inflationary economy. As a result of uncontrolled increases in many of the
contracted services, the Administration in preparing its budget is mindful of the economic burdens to the citi-
zenry of the area and, hence, is making every effort to hold assessments to a minimum and, at the same time, main-
tain a quality level of education for its students.
A number of commendations were awarded to the school, particularly our effort in the United Fund, havingac-
complished the greatest gain of any educational institution in the area; its participation in civic activities; the blood
bank; visits to Nursing Homes and Hospitals; participating in community-sponsored fairs; and complimentary references
by various agencies to the teaching methodology and accomplishments of the students. As a result of a five-year ac-
creditation, a two-year follow-up report is required this Spring. The report will consist of additional accomplish-
ments since the original five-year accreditation and future plans.
Through the efforts of representatives of local industry, the School Committee, Administration, Staff and Stu-
dents, we at "Monty Tech" have evidencedone of the highest caliber educational institutions in the Common-
wealth. Mr. Charles F. Valera is the elected representative from the Town of Lunenburg.

No School Signals - 1976-77
The decision to close schools is made by the Superintendent of Schools after consulting with
the Highway Superintendent and the Police Department to determine the condition of the roads.
The practice is to close schools only in case of extreme weather conditions or unforeseen
emergency; therefore, when schools are in session on stormy days, parents are urged to exercise
their personal judgment as to the wisdom of sending their children to school.
The following schedule of NO-SCHOOL SIGNALS will be in effect for the current school year:
TWO SETS OF TWO (2) BLASTS ON THE HORN AT THE CENTRAL FIRE STATION INDICATES
NO SCHOOL AT THE FOLLOWING TIMES FOR THE GROUPS INDICATED:
6:30 AM
Grades 8 through 12
7:15 AM
Grades Kdg. through 7
10:00 AM
PM Session, Kindergarten
Announcements will be carried on the regular broadcasts of Radio Stations WEIM & WFGL, Fitch-
burg; WLMS, Leominster; as well as WTAG & WSRS (Stereo 96) in Worcester; and WBZ in Boston, as
promptly as the decision is made.
The cancelling of school sessions requires the completion of numerous separate telephone calls
as rapidly as possible. The crowding of telephone circuits can mean that the signal center or bus
contractors might not be notified on time. Parents can help by not allowing their children to make
unnecessary calls.

Annual Report of Superintendent of Schools
It is a pleasure to submit my Seventh Annual
Report as Superintendent of Schools in Lunenburg.
This is the 107th such report preserved in the
Town Records and covers the bi-centennial year
ending December 31, 1976.
SUMMARY OF HIGHLIGHTS - - 1976
This year, the annual reports of other school
administrators will provide the reader with a
comprehensive review of significant events in the
Lunenburg schools during 1976. It is not my in-
tention to restate these happenings but because
of their special significance I would like to
briefly underscore a few of them.
The herculean effort of GERMANY '77 defi-
nitely represents that unique spirit of pride,
cooperation, and enthusiasm that sets Lunenburg
schools and music program apart from the rest of
the schools in the area. At this writing,
slightly over 100 students are scheduled to
depart for Germany on April 14, 1977. In cele-
bration of Lunenburg's 250th anniversary let the
Town Records show that students, parents, faculty
and all townspeople have united around this pro-
- Dr. William R. Holland -
ject and take great pride in sending these young
musicians to Germany as representatives of our
community. Special gratitude has to go to the
Lunenburg Music Aiders who have worked tirelessly in support of this project.
Another milestone was reached with the
resignation of Dick Mulligan as Varsity Track
Coach after twenty years at the helm. Many times
a man's accomplishments are not realized until he
steps down. This is the case with "Mull". New
England, state and regional champions and cham-
pionships, undefeated stretches that spanned
several years, the development of a spring pro-
gram that includes over 150 boys and girls are
but a few of Dick's achievements. In many quar-
ters throughout the state, the name, "Lunenburg"
is synonymous with track. Although he is stepping
down to devote more time to his dual responsibil-
ities as Athletic Director and Supervisor of
Physical Education, you can be assured that Dick's
track legacy will live on for many years.
GILL

opening day ceremonies this year for their out-
standing service to Lunenburg schools. Dick
Carey, our High School Principal, has completed
thirty years of service to Lunenburg as a teacher
and administrator. Highly respected by his
teachers and known for his fairness and sense of
humor, Mr. Carey was also honored by the Class of
1976 as they dedicated their yearbook to him last

ENROLLMENT ANALYSIS - - The Future
This past fall, Dr. John Marshall, an educational consultant who at one time was Associate
State Commissioner of School Building Assistance, completed a comprehensive pupil enrollment study
and projection for Lunenburg. Dr. Marshall reviewed records and materials from the Town Hall and
the State Department of Education, had discussions with representatives from Montachusett Regional
Planning Commission, examined past enrollment studies, and personally toured our facilities. The
following is an excerpt from his report:
"A safer conclusion (referring to his charts and statistics that reflect downward
enrollment trends) is the more general one indicated on the charts: grades 1-4 had
about 180 pupils per grade in 1965, have about 150 per grade now, and are likely to have
about 125 per grade by 1982; grades 5-8 have now about 180 per grade, and are likely
to have about 150 per grade by 1980 and 125 per grade by 1985; grades 9-12 are likely
to drop from about 180 per grade now to about 150 per grade by 1984.
"All the projection lines point downward. Does this mean that Lunenburg will have
no further school housing problems? Not at all!
"While the immediate future seems likely to go in the directions indicated, a long-
range forecast looks different. Remember that the projections reported here reflect
declining birth figures during a period of economic stringency. The Montachusett
Regional Planning Commission forecasts for Lunenburg a growth in population from 8158
in 1975 to about 10, by 1985. Such growth would mean that school enrollment, in-
stead of continuing to drop, would turn upward in the 1980s.
"Among the factors encouraging community growth are access to jobs, shopping, and
recreation; good schools; and a liberal amount of residentially-suitable land. Lunen-
burg has these, and is bound to share in area population growth when it occurs.
"Specially pertinent to current problems is what these projections mean for utili-
zation of Lunenburg's school plant facilities. A change under consideration would have
the elementary school housing grades K-4, the annex grade five, the middle school
grades 6-8, and the high school grades 9-12. These moves are quite feasible. The need
for establishing staff and community readiness suggests the fall of 1978 for them.
"I would wrge continued liaison, through the local Planning Board, with the Monta-
chusett Regional Planning Commission, so that Lunenburg can be aware of regional
changes that will have local impact; and I would urge careful review of enrollment
change and periodic updating of projections in the light of new factors that may come
to light."
The enrollment picture becomes even more revealing when you compare October 1st enrollment
for the last six years with Dr. Marshall's projections for the next six years:

Contributing to this decline has been a decreasing rate of in-migration of families with
children and a sharp drop in the birth rate. For example, in 1961 there were 142 children born to
Lunenburg parents, in 1967 there were 112 children born, in 1973 there were 81 newborns, in 1975
there were 70 born, and in 1976 there were only 58 new births.
These conditions have a substantial effect upon future enrollment as evidenced by Dr. Mar-
shall's projections for kindergarten and first grade enrollment:

Another significant indicator of enrollment decline is to compare the peak enrollment years
with present enrollment levels. For example, in 1970 there were 833 students in kindergarten
through grade four. This year there were 709 students in those grades and next year we should
drop below 700 students. In 1972-73, there were 581 students in the Middle School. This year
there were 528 students and next year Middle School enrollment should range between 480 to 490
students.
As Dr. Marshall suggests, hope-
fully pupil enrollment will begin to
level off and then start an upward
turn in the 1980s. Since I view the
present and predicted enrollment
decline as an opportunity to reduce
costs, improve. efficiency, and re-
solve several organizational problems
we have lived with for years, I have
recommended the following actions to
the School Committee:
1. Starting with the 1978-79
school year, I have recom-
mended that the 8th grade be
relocated in the Middle
School and the 5th grade be-
come part of the elementary
school. The Annex could
either house the 5th grade
will there be more students in this
or serve as an early child-
picture in the 1980s?
hood education center housing
kindergartens and grade one.
In any event, these issues and others need to be carefully studied next year, and a plan
of implementation developed for the 1978-79 school year.
2. Because of shifts in enrollment in 1978-79, I will recommend that the Middle School and
High School students be on the same time schedules and ride the same buses. The elemen-
tary school time schedule could then be shortened accordingly without affecting the length
of the school day in the other two schools.
3. With enrollment decline currently being felt most markedly in the elementary grades, the
School Committee has approved my recommendation to eliminate two teaching positions and
one administrative position next year. The elimination of the two half-time administrative
assistant positions at the Middle School also led to an administrative reorganization that
realigned Burt Goodrich's job duties. Beginning July 1st, Dr. Goodrich will occupy a dual
role spending three-fifths of his time as administrative assistant to the superintendent
and two-fifths of his time as assistant principal at the Middle School. Although we have
eliminated six staff positions in the past two years, we have also added a second speech
therapist, a teacher/counselor in the High School, and a remedial reading teacher in the
Middle School during this time. Enrollment decline, therefore, has allowed us the oppor-
tunity to reduce overall staff costs while at the same time meeting several pressing and
critical personnel needs that have existed for some time.

SCHOOL COMMITTEE GOAL STATEMENTS - -
This past fall, the School Committee participated in a twelve-hour workshop for the purpose
of drafting a list of goal statements and priorities for the Lunenburg School System. Attempting
to establish "benchmarks" for education in Lunenburg, the School Committee is currently soliciting
reactions to its goal statements from students, teachers, parents, and administrators. Since
a
management-by-objectives approach to personnel evaluation is being followed by all supervisory
personnel in our school system, the School Committee felt it was important to articulate a broad
statement of goals which would help set directions for education in Lunenburg schools. With this
thought in mind, the following draft was prepared by the School Committee -
FUNDAMENTAL ASSUMPTIONS:
To provide guidelines and principles from which decisions can be made and upon
which judgments about the success of various parts of the system can be judged, the
following convictions are proposed. It is accepted that a school system's effective-
ness depends on the quality of the interaction of all of its components--school
committee, administration, staff, students, and community. The (ideal) school system
provides a climate of mutual respect in which members of the system can achieve the max-
imum potential for human growth.
Specifically, the characteristics and features of the components may be defined as
follows -
LEARNING ENVIRONMENT AND STUDENT:
The learning environment should provide for motivating, challenging, demanding, and
exciting experiences in enjoyable, safe surroundings. It should recognize, encourage,
and provide for the uniqueness of each student's needs and capabilities.
THE COMMUNITY AND PARENTS:
The community should be involved, aware, informed, and supportive. It should feel a
sense of pride and reward for its support.
THE SCHOOL COMMITTEE:
As one component of the system, the committee recognizes that it should be responsi-
ble to the needs of parents, students, and the community-at-large.
THE ADMINISTRATION:
The administrative staff of the system should consist of competent individuals who
are sensitive, flexible, compassionate people. They should constantly seek effective,
efficient, economical means to operate the personnel and physical components of the
system. They should also be open and receptive to suggestion and initiate communica-
tion in their efforts to detect and correct observed weaknesses.
THE TEACHING STAFF:
The members of the staff should be perceptive, competent, and committed. They should
express a sensitive, positive and mature attitude. They should be responsible and
humane in their actions. Educators should be receptive to criticism. Their treatment
of students should be fair, impartial and caring.
THE CURRICULUM:
The curriculum of the system should provide
a body of basic skills: in communication, thinking, problem-solving and
decision-making, for life-long learning
opportunity to develop a marketable skill or talent
tools for becoming an effective, responsible member of society
learning to be self-sufficient and understanding of practical, basic economic
principles
CONCLUSION - -
In this my Seventh Annual Report as Superintendent of Schools, my thanks go to the School
Committee and to members of our school staff, particularly my fellow administrators, teachers,
and members of the non-teaching staff. It is their collective effort which contributes to the
-101-
===PAGE 104===
fine reputation Lunenburg schools enjoy. Special thanks must also go to my capable secretary, Mrs.
Ginny Welch, who in a surprise move and after waiting 33 years, finally received her Gold "L" dur-
ing last year's graduation ceremonies.
To townspeople, I thank you again for your continued support. We are particularly thankful
for those people who volunteer their time and service to our schools.
As someone told me seven years ago, "We have a fine school system in Lunenburg and we want
to keep it that way." I told this person that I couldn't agree with them more; however, if you
are to maintain that excellent reputation you always have to try to be better. Seven years later
in this our bi-centennial year of 1976, I feel exactly the same way.
Report of Administrative Assistant
IS THE SCHOOL COMMITTEE AFFECTED BY CITIZENS'
OPINIONS?
In the spring of 1975 the Lunenburg School
Committee sent a questionnaire to all community
residents who were seventeen years of age and
older. The intent of that undertaking, as stated
by the School Committee, was to determine what
Lunenburg residents wanted in and from their
schools.
In the Annual Town Report last year, it was
reported that initial information received from
the questionnaire did change School Committee
opinions (both individually and as a group) to
be more in line with majority Community opinion.
Further, indepth study of the School Committee
over a six-month period of time which included
the fall of 1975 and spring of 1976 found that
School Committee actions and perspectives were
also influenced by data gathered in the ques-
tionnaire.
A log of School Committee meetings showed
that several actions related to questionnaire
data were taken in regular, executive sessions
and negotiation meetings. In total, 118 School
Committee actions occurred in forty different
- Dr. Burton E. Goodrich, Jr. -
School Committee settings. The actions were
related to a wide range of topics (i. e., staff-
ing, budget, class size, negotiations, student
attendance, guidance, communications, etc.). It appeared that the comprehensive nature of the
questionnaire provided opportunities for application of data to a broad spectrum of School Commit-
tee business.
Several School Committee perspectives about the questionnaire process and data emerged in the
six-month study. Following are some examples:
1. In general, opinions expressed by the Community were expected by the School Committee.
However, in some cases, Community opinions did identify topics of special interest
and concern that were not expected by the School Committee.
2. The School Committee mostly concerned itself only with the majority opinion although
the Committee was knowledgeable of different opinions expressed by sub-populations in
the Community.
-102-
===PAGE 105===
3. The School Committee held the perspective that the opinions of citizens who attended
the last Annual Town Meeting were not totally representative of the total community.
4. The School Committee felt that questionnaire data should be seriously considered by
them in their decision-making process but the data should not dictate their actions.
The School Committee did not perceive the questionnaire data as being a mandate for
action.
5. In most cases of School Committee decision-making that involved questionnaire data,
the questionnaire data was not a primary factor in influencing the School Committee.
Rather, the questionnaire data was considered as a part of the School Committee's
"total package" of information.
6. The School Committee perceived the questionnaire as an important and successful
public relations/communication process.
7. The School Committee perceived the Community Questionnaire data as a vote of confi-
dence for the Town's educational program and the School Committee.
The findings of this study
provide several implications.
First and foremost, they indicate
that the School Committee did take
substantive action to become more
representative of and responsive
to its citizens. And, that this
action was taken with the accom-
panying knowledge that all had
been provided an opportunity for
input.
The study pointed out that
the Lunenburg School System, which
was given an overall good to
excellent rating by its community,
still did possess areas of concern
to the citizens. In some cases
the concerns were known by the
School Committee and in other
cases the concerns were new infor-
mation. The School Committee was
sensitive to this feedback and did
Homecoming Festivities - 1976
move to improve concern areas.
Knowledge of the opinions of all
people coupled with a "vote of confidence" for their schools and the School Committee's efforts,
seemed to renew the direction, emphasis, and purpose of both the School Committee and the Superin-
tendent. This, over the long haul, could well be the most important outcome of the entire ques-
tionnaire project.
ENERGY CONSERVATION IN LUNENBURG PUBLIC SCHOOLS - 1
With the acute rise in oil prices in 1972 and the subsequent cost increases in other energy
sources, the Lunenburg School Department began energy conservation measures. Some immediate steps
that were taken included certain mechanical changes, maintenance procedures, and education of stu-
dents, staff and community regarding facility use. Longer term energy saving goals included bud-
geting for certain energy saving maintenance services, equipment, controls, etc.
Some highlights of energy conservation measures taken during the past four and one-half
years are as follows:
1. Service and maintenance of heating system controls has been budgeted on a regular,
yearly basis for the Elementary, Middle and High schools. As a result of this, all
fresh air intakes were adjusted, per State regulation, from about 30% - 40% down to
-103-
===PAGE 106===
10%; all controls were adjusted for
accuracy and efficiency and where
it was needed, controls were
replaced and updated; new units for
MBC
measuring outside temperature and
regulating hot water flow from the
boilers were installed; and regular
maintenance systems have been
established for preventive care of
control systems.
2. Boilers, especially those which
heat the Elementary School and High
School, were serviced, repaired,
and updated to include new electri-

Report of High School Principal
- Richard J. Carey -
- Philip M. Crouse -
PRINCIPAL
ASSISTANT
Some changes have been made in academic requirements and some new programs have been imple-
mented at Lunenburg High School this year.
CAREER INFORMATION CENTER - -
A special Career Information Center (c. I. C.) was opened in a renovated storage room as an
adjunct of the guidance department. Mrs. Jean Killeen, Business Education Department Head, has
received special training and has shown interest in this area and along with Mr. Falcone, Guidance
Counselor, demonstrated leadership in the center's implementation. The center is staffed by
twenty to twenty-five faculty members on a voluntary basis.
With a greater need and interest in occupations after graduation, the C. I. C. was instituted
in order that the students may be better prepared for the important selection of career or occupa-
tional preferences. The center is designed to assist students in the exploration and research of
career possibilities based on their individual skills, knowledges, and interests.
Information is available in the C. I. C. covering over three hundred occupational areas in-
cluding the professions and trades, as well as service and governmental organizations. The range
of career preferences is very broad and new materials are being added continually.
The center is open to all high school students every school day with professional assistance
from teachers available during the morning hours.
-105-
===PAGE 108===
ALTERNATIVE RESOURCE ROOM - -
The Alternative Resource Room (A. R. R.) was designed by a group of local administrators,
counselors, and teachers, and implemented in the high school this year for the purpose of helping
those students who have difficulty adjusting to the school environment and requirements. The
behavior exhibited by these students includes truancy, repeated disruption of classes, defiance,
inability to follow through on tasks and assignments and the problems of coping with large group
situations.
In order to try to remediate the aforementioned problems, the staff of the A. R. R. carries
out the following activities: counseling, academic tutoring, and small group discussions focusing
on certain common identifiable patterns of behavior.
As students are referred to the A. R. R. by teachers, counselors, or administrators, teachers
of the students are consulted to determine the extent and nature of the disruptive behavior and to
obtain academic information for tutorial help. Teachers are frequently consulted in order that
the A. R. R. teacher/counselor might determine whether the student is progressing satisfactorily
when not assigned to the A. R. R. room.
Students spend from two periods per week to two periods per day in the resource room, depend-
ing on the remediation deemed necessary. An exception to this procedure occurs when those students
whose behavior warrants an in-school suspension are assigned to the A. R. R. for three full days.
The student is counseled during the in-school suspension and all of his assignments are
obtained from his regular teachers and completed with tutorial help in the resource room during
the period of suspension.
In many cases, suspension out of school is non-corrective and frequently generates greater
antagonistic behavior patterns. Hence, the new efforts and trials of some in-school suspensions.

An evaluation will be undertaken at the conclusion of the programs and if satisfactory, it is
expected that Alcohol Education will become a vital, formal, and required part of our total health
education program.

ACADEMIC ACHIEVEMENT -
The Class of 1977 has received recognition for outstanding scholastic achievement this year
from the National Merit Scholarship Corporation. As a result of tests administered in 1976 to
over a million high school juniors in the fifty states, three of our students were recognized as
semi-finalists, placing them in the top half of one percent of the senior class students within
the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Upon the local school's recommendation based on academic
achievement, in all probability these students will become finalists. The students so honored for
their own scholastic application, interest, and attitude are: Grant Bergman, Carolyn Heimberg,
and Jeffrey Janell.
In addition, seven other senior students received Letters of Commendation as a result of
their scores on the same series of tests. This recognition placed these students among the top
two percent of all secondary school seniors throughout the country. This number of students from
a public high school of our size is extremely significant. Lunenburg High School has never before
placed more than three or four students in this national scholarship competition in any one year.
This outstanding accomplishment brings great credit to the students, the school staff, and the
community.
NEW GRADUATION CREDIT REQUIREMENT - -
After a year of faculty study and discussion, the School Committee approved an increase in
the number of academic credits needed for graduation. Effective with the Class of 1980 to be
declared eligible for graduation, high school students will need to complete nineteen (19) units
including the successful completion of four years of English and the completion of one year of
American history and four years of physical education. Eighteen (18) units will remain the
requirement for the classes of 1977, 1978, and 1979.
-107-
===PAGE 110===
GERMANY '77 - -
During 1976, the Lunenburg Music Aiders, the parent support organization for our student
musicians, launched the ambitious project of sending our high school band and chorus on a per-
forming tour of Germany during the period of April 14 to April 23, 1977. Highlighting the trip
will be a visit to Luneburg, a German community that might well be considered our sister city in
Europe. A concert by our musical ensembles and a cordial civic exchange will be the feature of
our visit to this beautiful Medieval community. Our trip throughout Germany is being considered
by many as an integral part of our own community's 250th birthday.
The performances of our band and chorus will afford our young musicians with the priceless
opportunity of representing their town, state, and country in the wholesome manner that music
provides. They will also reap the many cultural benefits inherent in travel itself. In addition
to the performances, these young people will also see a large part of Germany, visiting many
historical sites and enjoying new experiences in another country that make one a better world
citizen.
The Lunenburg Music Aiders have been working diligently on this project since last summer and
are now very close to the realization of their goal. The community owes a debt of gratitude to
the co-chairpersons of the Ways and Means Committee-Mrs. Carl Smith and Mrs. Walter Gendron.
CONCLUSION - -
In closing, I wish to express the students', the staff's, and my appreciation for the very
responsible and dedicated work of my secretary, Mrs. Dorothy Benjamin. Mrs. Benjamin has retired
after fifteen years' of work in the high school office. She had the qualities needed in excess:
firmness, understanding, patience, tolerance, tact, responsibility, and knowledge. In working
with over seventy adults and eight hundred to nine hundred students each year she was known to be
one who never made a mistake. To work in a high school office today one should be entitled to
double retirement; to be a high school principal's secretary, and especially mine, one should be
entitled to triple retirement. Sincerely, we all wish "Dot" and Walter many years of well-merited,
happy and travel-full leisure.
LET FREEDOM RINO

And what of our product? In the Town of Lunenburg it might be said that education is our

Report of Special Needs Director
"Some people make the world more
special just by being in it"
- G. W. Douglas
In my second year with the Lunenburg
schools, I would like to report the progress the
community has made in serving children with
special needs.
In accord with federal and state legisla-
tion and whenever possible in the interest of
the child, the educational placement of the stu-
dent is within the school district. That basic
goal is further supported by the directed educa-
tional practice that children shall be inte-
grated with other children who may be different
in some way. When the placement of a student is
out-of-district, such placement is as close to
home as possible and this is clearly indicated
in this legislation and is implemented in the
required approval of certain placements by state
and regional boards.
SPECIAL NEEDS POPULATION - -
In the laws of Massachusetts, Chapter 766
of the Acts of 1972 requires the school system
to evaluate children with possible special needs
between the ages of 3 - 21 who reside within the
community. Estimates of special needs children
in the United States and other countries range
- Floyd Benitz -
from 10% to 22%. In Massachusetts, the Depart-
ment of Education has set 12% as the anticipated
proportion of youngsters in each community who
require evaluation and services. According to that guideline and based on the total Lunenburg
schools population of 2094, we may expect to evaluate and serve 251 special needs students in a
-110-
===PAGE 113===
year. At the October 1976 report date, we were instructing 147 special needs students. Though
less than the guideline this is more than a 100% increase over the previous year.
The State guideline does not consider the contrasts in communities across the Commonwealth.
In consideration of the relatively favorable economic and health conditions in Lunenburg, it is
expected that the special needs population will level off at the minimum level of 10% or approxi-
mately 200 pupils.
In addition to the remarkable increase in number of children served this year, it must be
noted that faculty who have regular teaching schedules with children are at the same time studying
new children, consulting with teachers and parents, monitoring students' school performance,
assisting parents in home instruction, directing volunteers and advancing their own training. This
special education staff is steadily evaluating children referred throughout the year.
The children of Lunenburg now being served generally have moderate to severe special needs or
combinations of difficulties warranting service. As the programs move forward and embrace about
200 students, it is expected that moderate to mild special needs will be identified. There is a
team evaluation along with parent input to determine special need and even a mild special need is
by definition a special need to be followed by an educational plan and service. That service is
usually through the regular classroom teacher involving consultations and modifications in the
regular program.
CORE EVALUATION - -
No educational legislation in Massachusetts history has generated as much documentation and
record-keeping in order to serve the child according to the law. In this respect, compliance with
the law has the net effect of either employing sufficient professional personnel to prepare reports
or of losing some direct service to the child in order to prepare records and reports and attend
child evaluation meetings. Similarly, the kinds of expertise and number of persons represented at
such meetings is directed by regulations and frequently this has the effect of a minimum of five
staff hours in meeting with a parent to plan for a child.
There is hope that required state procedures will be reduced as a result of study andhearings
this year. Meanwhile, staff will work with the many forms that require professional attention and
serve as many children as possible.
EARLY INTERVENTION - -
It is generally accepted among health and education professionals that the earlier children
with special needs are identified, the better the opportunity of meeting the special needs. In
accord with the law and the practice of early identification, we screen three and four-year olds
every spring wherein the parents think the child may have special needs. A subsequent comprehen-
sive evaluation of selected pre-schoolers results in services and placement according to individual
need.
PLACEMENTS OF CHILDREN - -
Following a core evaluation and meeting with the parents, a child is planned for services or
it may be determined that no special need is evident. The number of students receiving services
were distributed within the district in the recent two-year period as follows:
October 1975
October 1976

The increase in services within the district is reflected in the fact that more children have
been evaluated according to the law and found in need of service.
-111-
===PAGE 114===
OUT-OF-DISTRICT PLACEMENTS - -
Placements out of the school district are necessary for a few children with physical and men-
tal problems of such severity that the condition occurs infrequently in the population. Particu-
larly evident are the birth defect children and young people injured in accidents. These children
may require clinical treatment or rehabilitation services or a long-term pediatric nursing home.
Within Chapter 766, the community is responsible for the cost of education of out-of-district
placements. A comparison of the number of children receiving services out-of-district is as
follows:
October 1975: 13
October 1976: 7
The reduction in these placements is most consistent with Chapter 766 objectives to "main-
stream" youngsters and provide service as close to home as possible. Similarly, the planning and
employment of appropriate personnel in the district reduces the need for out-of-district placement
for several students and reduces the costs of transportation. However, the reduction in number of
out-of-district placements in any given year may not result in less expense because of cost of a
special setting for those students who are new or changing placements.
DEVELOPMENTS IN SPECIAL EDUCATION - -
Following a comprehensive search and screening of applicants last summer, new personnel were
employed in speech therapy, learning disabilities (part-time) and high school resource teaching.

With the advent of adolescence, students become more sensitive and intense about their prob-

Report of Middle School Principal
The Turkey Hill Middle School
faculty greeted 529 students on
opening day of the 1976-77 school
year. Grade 5 had 161 students,
grade 6 had 167 students and grade
7 lead the way with 201 students.
Since opening day on September 8,
1976 ,we have had students transfer
to other school systems and have
had new students enter. However,
our enrollment has remained quite
stable and now stands at 528.
OPEN HOUSE - -
Again this year we held an
open house on November 3, 1976.
The turnout of parents, grand-
parents and friends was most
gratifying. We estimated that
there were approximately 250 people
in attendance throughout the day.
We do not put on any special
programs during Open House because
- Floyd F. Barney -

PRINCIPAL

The Middle School faculty and administration are grateful to the P-TO members who served
refreshments and greeted our many visitors.
SCIENCE TRAIL - -
Through the untiring efforts and dedication of Mr. Robert Saudelli, we now have a Science
Trail at the Middle School. Mr. Saudelli, a sixth grade teacher, donated his time throughout the
summer months digging post holes, stringing wire, cleaning up debris and performing many other
chores to develop a viable outdoor science program for Middle School students.
The Turkey Hill Middle School is surrounded by land which is quite beautiful. The fields and
woods bordering the building if used thoughtfully and carefully, can be a real aid to learning.
The trail, once completed, will be a permanent school resource designed for use in all four
seasons by grades 5, 6 and 7. Use of the trail will not be limited to Science classes since any
curricular area may visit the trail under teacher supervision.
Some of the work yet to be completed includes an entrance sign, trail signs on posts, and an
outdoor classroom with log benches. Written material including a seasonal trail identification
guide for teacher use and a trail activity curriculum guide for grades 5, 6 and 7 must also be
produced.
Once completed, the nature trail should prove to be a valuable resource in helping Middle
School students gain a better understanding and appreciation of their natural environment.
DISPLAY CASE - -
Through the efforts of Mr. Robert Okerman and his maintenance staff, we now have a beautiful
display case in the Middle School. The display case enables us to exhibit many projects that the
children undertake during the school year.

BI - CENTENNIAL CELEBRATION - Elementary School
- June 4, 1976
One month before our country's bi-centennial celebration, students and teachers of the Passios
Elementary School celebrated by holding a Bi-Centennial Fair. Members of the Bi-Centennial Fair
Steering Committee planned a day that was both fun-filled and educational. Members of the community
who were gifted in "old time" crafts or who had antique collections were present to share themwith
the children. Pupils in Grade 3A made shoe box exhibits depicting Revolutionary War events such as
Paul Revere's Ride; Washington Crossing the Delaware; and the Boston Tea Party. Students also made
Paul Revere lanterns out of juice cans and participated in a Bi-Centennial breakfast featuring
Indian slapjacks. Spinning, weaving and dyeing demonstrations were also conducted throughout the
day. Members of the fourth grade completed a unit on "America's Bi-Centennial" and many activities
were performed by the children including role-playing and art activities. Boys as well as girls,
with needle and thread in hand, became tailors and seamstresses. The boys made vests and the girls
made long skirts which they wore for the culmination of the unit, "A Happy 200th Birthday America
Party".
-117-
===PAGE 120===
Report of Elementary School Principal
- Paul E. Ralston -
- Robert Finneron -
PRINCIPAL
ASSISTANT
The opening day enrollment figures for the 1976-77 school year totaled 709 children in kinder-
garten through grade four. This represented a decline of 33 students from the previous years.
Since the 1973-74 school year our enrollment has shown a decrease of approximately 100 stu-
dents. As a result of this change in our population, we have eliminated teaching positions in both
the first and second grade and will be eliminating a third grade position next year.
At present our class size averages are as follows:
Kindergarten - 20 students
Grade one
- 23 students
Grade two - 25 students
Grade three - 22 students
Grade four - 23 students
In addition to the above listed averages there is one small group class at each grade level
which averages sixteen students.
PERSONNEL CHANGES - -
The following personnel changes were made at the Passios School prior to the 1976-77 school
year:
- Mr. William LaClair replaced Mrs. Cross at the fourth grade level.
Mr. LaClair transferred to the Passios School after 2½ years of
teaching at the sixth grade in the Turkey Hill Middle School.
-118-
===PAGE 121===
- Mrs. Lynn Fletcher replaced Mrs. Barbara Weller as a Title I
remedial reading teacher. Mrs. Fletcher has six years of
previous teaching experience in Oneco, Connecticut.
- Mrs. Linda Powell has been employed as a speech therapist.
Mrs. Powell has a total of six years of experience in speech
pathology in New York and Connecticut.
- Mrs. Pauline Celona is serving as a part-time reading
teacher filling a vacancy created by Marilyn Cormier's trans-
fer to grade five.
- Mrs. Gail Allen was employed as a part-time special educa-
tion tutor. Mrs. Allen has two years of experience working
with educationally handicapped children.
CURRICULUM - -
Three new programs were added to the ele-
mentary school curriculum this year. In the
area of language arts we have once again imple-
mented a formal handwriting program. The Rine-
hart Handwriting System is being practiced in
kindergarten through grade four with a Rinehart
instructor teaching all students in grades one
and three. Grade one students are taught proper
formation of printed letters with a transition
to script writing being made in grade three.
Teachers at all grade levels are reinforcing
writing skills on a regular basis.
Mathematics For Individual Achievement was
introduced to all first and second grade stu-
dents. The content of this program emphasizes
computational skills through a laboratory or
"hands on" approach and serves as an appropriate
introduction to the Individualized Math System
(I. M. S.) program used in grades three through
six. Reinforcement through practice and drill
activities is also stressed.
All students in grades two through four are
using the Modular Activities Program in Science.
This is a new series which combines both an
experiential and a text approach to science
"Ah yes, I remember it well."
instruction. The program parallels the concepts
Garet Sims, Grade 2.
developed in the S. C. I. S. science curriculum
currently in use at the Middle School level.
STAFF DEVELOPMENT/IN-SERVICE TRAINING - -
In teaching, as in other professions, in-service education is an extremely necessary aspect of
professional improvement. Throughout the year the staff at the Passios School has been enthusias-
tically involved in a series of monthly staff development workshops. Workshop topics have included:
"Developing Classroom Environments", "Creating Learning Games", "Teaching with Audio-Visual Aids",
"Dealing with Effects of Death and Divorce", and "Introducing New Curriculum". Special thanks go
to the teachers who have assisted in planning and presenting the various programs.
COMMUNITY INVOLVEMENT - -
Throughout the year the staff and administration have enjoyed a close working relationship
with parents and community agencies. I would particularly like to thank Fire Chief Deming and
-119-
===PAGE 122===
Police Chief Hobbs for assisting us with a school-wide safety program. I would also like to thank
Mrs. Susan Sanderson, Mrs. Cliff Miller, Mrs. Diane Hambleton, Mrs. Judy Davis and the many other
parents who have given their time and effort on behalf of the school. Our volunteer help has
enabled the school to:
- improve instruction in the classroom
- establish better communications through our newsletter and
parent conferences
- increase educational opportunities by chaperoning or pro-
viding transportation for field trips.
Volunteer efforts have had a most positive impact on the quality of education provided by the
elementary school.
Pictured above are participants in monthly elementary staff development workshops.
TOP LEFT CLOCKWISE: Arthur Pinsoneault, media specialist at McKay School,
instructs Mrs. Conrad in use of video tape and televising live performances.
Mrs. Shinner demonstrates how to construct a math game.
Mrs. O'Malley (Shawn's mother) helps Mrs. Kibling paint classroom wall.
Bob Finneron checks out sound filmstrip projector.
CONCLUSION - -
In conclusion, I would like to thank the entire staff, especially Bob Finneron, Assistant
Principal, and Shirlie Ruggles, School Secretary, for their continued support and excellent ser-
vice to the children.

1976-77 school year
Barbara A. WELLER
- Maternity leave
Lynn M. Fletcher
Retirement
Completing almost fifteen years of ser-
vice as a member of the secretarial staff, Dot
retired from her position as Secretary to the
high school principal effective December 31st.
She had served as Senior Clerk/Typist in the
high school office from 1962 to June 2, 1969
at which time she was promoted to the position
of Principal Clerk with duties as school
treasurer and principal's secretary. Dot was
one of those conscientious, dedicated, loyal
and dependable people who was held in high
esteem by all members of the school staff.
Her sensitivity, understanding, even tempera-
ment, zest for life and sense of humor were
qualities that contributed greatly to her
success as a school secretary.
On December 9th, the secretarial staff
honored Dot by treating her to dinner and pre-
senting her with a camera that surely will be
put to a great deal of use as Dot loves to
travel.
On December 18th, an Open House was held
where a large gathering of friends including
present and former staff members presented Dot
with a "live" money tree to show their appre-
ciation for her many years of service, her
friendship and her untiring efforts on behalf
DOROTHY E. BENJAMIN
of the Lunenburg Public Schools.
(April 16, 1962 - December 31, 1976)

staff of four registered nurses are also
URSING
responsible for the visiting nurse ser-
vices to the town.
REPORT
Janet Pace is the Nursing Supervisor;
Shirley Carpenter is the full-time nurse
at the Passios Elementary School; and
there are two part-time nurses--Jean
Hobbs at the Senior High School and Marie
Richards at the Turkey Hill Middle School.
Dr. Joel Neuschatz is the School Physi-
cian.
The focus of the school health program is on preventive health measures and early detection
of possible health problems. Vision and hearing screening is done annually on all students, with
parents being notified of any failures; and with referral forms from the doctors being returned to
the school SO we will best know how to help the child. Physical examinations are required on all
children entering school; on all students involved in the junior-senior high sports programs; on
students being evaluated under Chapter 766; on students with frequent complaints or absences; and
transferring students. Pre-school students are also screened, i. e., all those entering kinder-
garten the following fall, and any three or four-year old for which a parent requests a screening.
When necessary, home visits are made by the nurses concerning physical problems and Chapter 766
follow-ups. The nurses are always available for parent conferences either by phone or at school.
A new postural screening program is being
developed for 1977, under the guidance of the
Massachusetts Department of Public Health. Chil-
dren in grades 5 through 8 will be screened by
the physical education teachers and nurses for
any postural problems such as scoliosis. If
detected early, these conditions may be treated
easily and successfully.
Immunization and dental programs are
offered through the Nashoba Associated Boards of
Health to the following grades:
KINDERGARTEN: M-M-R (Measles, Mumps, Rubella)
GRADE 1
:
Trivalent Oral Polio Booster
:
Tetanus-Diptheria Booster
: Tuberculin Testing
GRADE 11
:
Tetanus Diptheria Booster
GRADES 2,
5, and 8
:
Dental Flouride program given
by dental hygienist
Mrs. Shirley Carpenter giving
To keep current with changes in the medical
first aid to an elementary student.
field and new programs available for the school
health program, the nurses are members of the
Massachusetts Nurses Association. Among continuing education programs attended this past year was
a six-session workshop given by the State for School Nurses and Coaches on sports injuries and
first aid. Dr. Keller, an Orthopedic Surgeon and Ms. Lezberg, a Physical Therapist, presented an
in-service program on scoliosis--detection, treatment and school screening methods. Mrs. Carpenter
and Mrs. Richards attended a presentation at Cutler Army Hospital on new methods of hearingscreen-
ing; Mrs. Hobbs and Mrs. Pace participated in a communications skills workshop sponsored by the
Special Needs Educational Collaborative.
-129-
===PAGE 132===
During the spring and fall of 1976, Mrs. Carpenter and Mrs. Hobbs offered a Red Cross Multi-
Media First Aid Course to the teachers in the Lunenburg schools. Thirty teachers took this eight-
hour course and received a Red Cross First Aid Certificate. In May of 1976, this Red Cross course
was given to the 10th grade by Mrs. Hobbs and Miss Claire Friedrich, science teacher at the high
school.
The entire nursing staff is looking forward to continuing and expanding the health program
offered to the students of Lunenburg during 1977.
